# Readiness for 10,000 daily active users

Assessment period: 15–19 October 2026 (Asia/Tashkent). Dated evidence below records changes and their verification boundaries.

**Status: NOT YET CERTIFIED for production at 10,000 DAU.** Current generation/scanning uses NVIDIA; earlier Gemini smoke results below are historical. Scoped generation and scan Auth/rate/entitlement admission, authoritative quota migration027 and bounded scheduled usage retention are deployed with live checks. Free allowances remain three Home swipes/five scans daily; new looks after trial require payment, and features stay visible. Latest controlled suite:1,695tests/141suites. The isolated three-repeat layered-photo run passed its expected category/color, but the subsequent concurrency test FAILED at10overlapping scans:9of10usable responses and8of10expected garment selections, with a NVIDIA429logged during the stage. Two- and five-request stages passed. These small bursts do not establish a provider limit or10,000DAU capacity. Billing is also unready: the public-SDK-key webhook authentication flaw is closed, and the dedicated backend secret and atomic subscription-sync integration are now deployed; RevenueCat-originated TEST delivery/authentication now pass; actual purchase lifecycle checks remain pending; new local checkout builds block known-unavailable verification before starting payment. Full provider/category accuracy, realistic mixed capacity, purchases, installed-device coverage and operations remain unproven. Prior read/write/media load results below retain their original scope.

**Write reliability remains unresolved:** the original two-minute 40-RPS wear-write test FAILED with 108 transport/decode failures and 647 dropped requests. A later instrumented repeat PASSED all 4,800 writes at the same rate/duration with exact counters and full cleanup. The original transport failure has not been explained or shown fixed. The separate Auth-cascade permission bug is fixed and verified over HTTP. All disposable fixtures are removed.

**Media readiness gap:** the earlier production snapshot showed 18 inline image fields totaling 15.89 MB and two device-local image references. The largest image field is 4.05 MB; no thumbnails are stored. Earlier small-URL fixture reads do not establish readiness for these actual media payloads. Storage-backed references and new-upload preview generation are implemented in source, with native codec checks recorded below. Legacy production media migration and end-user image-delivery capacity remain open.

**Latest distinct-JPEG media diagnostic:** at the same 2/5/10 RPS and a separately declared 40-read generator cap, both regions completed 170/170 reads, with zero failures/drops and exact bytes/hash on all 340 HTTP 200 responses (all CF-Cache-Status MISS). Frankfurt used 22 active reads and still has approximately 2.2-second p95 latency. The earlier 20-cap failure remains recorded; an earlier 334-upload preparation failure remains unexplained. This short, single-owner probe does not certify 10,000 DAU. All fixture accounts/objects/probes are independently cleaned.

## Measured local database capacity

Dataset: 10,000 synthetic users, 500,000 wardrobe items, 50,000 saved outfits. A transaction reads one owner's wardrobe, saved outfits and profile, resolves subscription tier, and increments a rate bucket with the service role. Ordinary reads run under authenticated RLS.

- 40 clients, four threads, 60 seconds, offered rate 200 transactions/second.
- 11,878 completed transactions; zero failed transactions.
- 198.06 transactions/second achieved.
- Reported transaction latency: mean 4.36 ms, p50 4.00 ms, p95 7.87 ms, p99 8.89 ms.
- Random workload exercised 6,972 distinct users from the seeded 10,000-user population.
- Debug logging was enabled; its overhead is included in these measurements.

This is a short local database benchmark. It does not measure internet latency, hosted database resources, Auth verification, Edge isolation/concurrency, uploads, image delivery, provider rate limits, AI correctness, or sustained peak traffic. Database transactions/second must not be presented as production HTTP capacity.

## Workload assumptions for HTTP verification

The user confirmed **10,000 daily active users**, not 10,000 concurrent users. Until real telemetry is available, the repeatable HTTP harness assumes:

- Two sessions per active user per day.
- Twelve HTTP requests per session: 240,000 requests/day.
- Twenty percent of requests during the busiest hour: 13.33 requests/second.
- Three times that peak: **40 requests/second** target.

These are explicit assumptions, not measured user behavior. Image requests, video uploads and AI request rates require separate workloads. The read harness uses catalog, wardrobe, saved-outfit and profile queries; validates response shape and ownership; treats 429s, other HTTP errors, invalid rows, insufficient achieved throughput and dropped load as failures. Thresholds are zero failures/drops, at least 95% of target throughput, p95 below one second and p99 below two seconds.

```sh
npm run load:plan
npm run test:load-harness

# Point only at an agreed staging project with ordinary test-user sessions.
# LOAD_USERS_FILE is private JSON: [{"id":"user UUID","accessToken":"user JWT"}, ...]
# Keep it outside the repository. A service-role key is not a user-session substitute.
LOAD_BASE_URL=https://STAGING.supabase.co \
LOAD_ANON_KEY=PUBLIC_ANON_KEY \
LOAD_USERS_FILE=/private/tmp/staging-users.json \
LOAD_REPORT_FILE=/private/tmp/staging-load-report.json \
LOAD_RPS=40 LOAD_DURATION_SEC=600 npm run load:readiness
```

The user explicitly authorized real production testing. The production baseline completed 150/150 requests at 5 requests/second with zero failures or dropped requests, p95 571 ms and p99 1,380 ms. The 600-second peak run completed 24,000 scheduled requests at 39.98 requests/second: 23,999 HTTP 200 responses and one network/JSON failure, zero dropped requests, p95 494 ms and p99 1,048 ms. **The peak run failed the zero-error acceptance criterion.** The original harness grouped transport and JSON errors; the failed request cannot be attributed to the server from this evidence. Diagnostics were subsequently extended to retain safe error type/cause codes for future runs.

The run used one temporary ordinary authenticated user and the read mix above; see `readiness-evidence/production-authenticated.json`. A single account does not demonstrate 10,000-user cardinality, sign-in throughput, populated private datasets, or provider capacity. Temporary account setup used the admin credential; measured reads used the normal user's token. The temporary load-test account was successfully deleted afterward (recorded in command output).

A subsequent, stronger populated test used **20 temporary ordinary authenticated users**, each with 50 wardrobe rows and five saved outfits (1,000 wardrobe rows / 100 outfits total). It completed all **4,800 requests** over 120 seconds with **zero failures, zero drops**, 39.85 requests/second achieved, p50 362 ms, p95 470 ms and p99 875 ms. All 20 sessions were signed out and their accounts deleted afterward. See `readiness-evidence/production-populated-load.json`. This passes the stated read-workload thresholds for that duration and dataset; it does not erase the earlier failure or establish 10,000-user cardinality or AI/storage capacity.

A bounded gateway-log query for the original run's time window returned 23,519 matching read entries, all HTTP 200. The log count does not cover every scheduled client request and cannot identify the original transport/JSON failure; no server-failure conclusion is justified from it alone.

The 1,000-row public catalog sample had images and positive prices; sample categories were tops, bottoms and shoes. It is not evidence of complete live inventory coverage or image URL availability.

## Live production functional findings

- Wardrobe, saved outfits and calendar wear logs passed 24 create/read/update/delete, persistence and two-account isolation assertions. Each test was confined to temporary users and explicitly created rows; both workflow accounts were deleted successfully. See `readiness-evidence/production-workflows.json`.
- **Fixed and verified in production:** before migration `026`, an ordinary temporary user PATCHed their own `profiles.subscription_tier` to `premium`, and a subsequent GET confirmed that persisted value. After deployment the same attack returns HTTP 403 and the stored tier stays `free`. Admin self-promotion, forged subscription insertion and trial reset also return 403; initial trial dates use server time and ordinary profile edits still succeed. See `production-profile-authority-before.json` and `production-profile-authority.json` in the evidence directory.
- **Fixed and verified in production:** `get_subscription_status` previously returned HTTP 404 / `PGRST202`; migration `029` installed the owner-only RPC and a fresh normal user now receives HTTP 200 with the correct free tier.
- The deployed generate-outfits, ai-process, try-on, ai-chat and analyze-outfit endpoints returned HTTP 401 to requests without Authorization. create-trip-plan and sync-subscription returned HTTP 404. These empty-body checks do not prove successful provider work or paid quota enforcement. See `readiness-evidence/production-edge-boundaries.json`.
- The repository's stored E2E password returned `invalid_credentials`; it was not reset. Temporary, explicitly marked test accounts were used instead.
- Representative provider requests used one temporary account with its first legitimate trial initialization. Chat returned text via NVIDIA in 4.15 seconds. A public catalog bottoms image was classified as pants / bottoms in 4.91 seconds. Outfit generation returned a complete top/bottom/shoes set in 24.61 seconds, but **reported `source: local`**, so the primary AI-generation criterion failed. Structural validity is not proof that the primary provider succeeded. The temporary account was deleted. See `readiness-evidence/production-ai-smoke.json`; these samples do not certify broad accuracy or provider throughput.
- The Supabase integration is connected and successfully queried production. The hosted ledger initially contained only `001`–`015`; direct schema inventory confirmed later manual changes, including Lite constraints, trial dates, usage counters and processed webhooks. Full migration push remains inappropriate. The live subscriptions table lacks the prepared billing sync's `auto_renew` column and needs an explicit compatibility change before that function is deployed.
- After the user completed macOS Keychain approval, migrations `026` and `029` were applied together with 5-second lock and 30-second statement timeouts, ledger registration and a PostgREST schema reload. The generated SQL SHA-256 was `33cedf68916289481e88bc60f11da308123f136d9967d31570247a5638c87725`; the CLI confirmed applied versions and the connected integration independently confirmed the trigger and RPC exist.
- The connected security scan found explicit anonymous grants on the rate-counter RPC, and public grants on login-state handlers and webhook pruning. Migration source `030_server_rpc_boundaries.sql` was applied through the integration as hosted version `20261015102421` / `server_rpc_boundaries`. Anonymous and ordinary authenticated calls to all four handlers now fail with 401/403, while the service role can still increment a temporary user's counter. Owner usage APIs remain authenticated. The analytics view now uses `security_invoker=true`, honoring underlying row policies. Follow-up advisories no longer report the [security-definer view error](https://supabase.com/docs/guides/database/database-linter?lint=0010_security_definer_view).
- Remaining hosted advisories include ten functions with mutable search paths, five anonymous-callable and eight authenticated-callable security-definer functions, and disabled leaked-password protection. Some public functions are intentional (for example, signup username availability); these require individual review. Performance advisories include 34 [RLS init-plan warnings](https://supabase.com/docs/guides/database/database-linter?lint=0003_auth_rls_initplan), 66 [multiple-permissive-policy findings](https://supabase.com/docs/guides/database/database-linter?lint=0006_multiple_permissive_policies), five unindexed foreign keys and one table without a primary key. Raw advisor results are preserved in the evidence directory.
- Documentation-based log queries resolved earlier query errors caused by unsupported wildcard selection. The [current log query guide](https://supabase.com/docs/guides/observability/advanced-log-filtering) specifies explicit columns and `event_message`. Live logs confirmed NVIDIA timeout/unparseable responses and HTTP 403 from both configured Gemini model attempts.
- A fresh real generation request exposed a deployed v64 crash **after NVIDIA successfully parsed an outfit**: `ReferenceError: providerAttempts is not defined`. The isolated v65 hotfix changes the success response to `providerAttempts: providerTrace`, initializes the trace before its early use, and adds four type-only callback annotations so the deployed snapshot passes Deno checking. The later v66 deployment adds only safe JSON-shape / finish-reason diagnostics; raw model/wardrobe content is not logged. JWT verification remains enabled. The full prepared local generation rewrite was not deployed. Before/after evidence and a reviewable patch are in `production-outfit-diagnostic-before.json`, `production-outfit-v65-attempt-1.json`, `production-outfit-v65-attempt-2.json`, and `production-outfit-hotfix.patch`. Both v65 samples returned HTTP 200 via the local path; the AI-success path remains unverified after deployment.
- Safe v66 diagnostics confirmed an NVIDIA response with `shape: invalid_json` and `finishReason: stop`, distinguishing malformed output from truncation. Version 67 lowered NVIDIA sampling temperature from 0.95 to 0.25 (matching the prepared local configuration). That request produced parseable JSON but failed the existing validator with an undefined item ID; its correction attempt timed out. Version 68 normalizes equivalent string / `itemId` / `item_id` references before validation, preserving canonical identifiers and keeping unknown IDs, duplicates, missing slots, ownership and style checks intact. Three boundary regressions pass and the deployed source plus helper pass Deno checking. The latest v68 request still timed out on NVIDIA and received Gemini 403 responses, then returned `source: local`. **Primary-AI acceptance still fails; these changes are not a provider reliability certification.** See `production-outfit-v66-diagnostic.json`, `production-outfit-v67-diagnostic.json` and the latest `production-outfit-diagnostic.json`.
- The user selected **Gemini** as the intended primary provider. A local Gemini-only path is prepared, with a structured response schema limiting item identifiers to the actual candidate pool; Deno checking passes. It has not replaced production's default routing. Version 69 added redacted Google error diagnostics; version 70 allows an authenticated request to select the already-supported Gemini provider without changing entitlements. The direct ordinary-trial probe still returned 403 for both configured Gemini models, and its account was deleted. Google's precise reason is **`API_KEY_SERVICE_BLOCKED`**, blocking the `GenerateContent` method; see `production-gemini-access.json` and `production-gemini-probe.json`. The user plans to correct the key restriction but has not confirmed that the change is saved. The first recorded Gemini 403 preceded these provider changes (15:03 Tashkent / 10:03 UTC). A separate ordinary-trial request later returned a genuine NVIDIA `source: ai` outfit; earlier apparently successful outfits do not establish successful Gemini access.
- A proposed synthetic premium-subscription probe was rejected by automatic approval review because it would change production billing/entitlement state beyond explicit approval. No subscription was created. That option was removed; subsequent tests use ordinary temporary trial accounts and provider selection, without subscription writes.

## Fixes prepared locally

- Connected six paid Edge Functions to authenticated per-user admission control. Database/configuration failures now stop paid work. Denials retain CORS headers and return appropriate retry timing.
- Added server-owned daily feature limits, independent endpoint metering, atomic quota increments and protected cleanup RPCs. Client-supplied unlimited limits, negative/oversized amounts and direct endpoint bypasses no longer grant usage after migration/deployment.
- Added authenticated immediate subscription synchronization, server-verified purchase/restore activation, translated pending-payment retry UI, and logout/account-switch protection. An authoritative status RPC ranks simultaneous subscriptions by tier and preserves lifetime expiry. Removed mock development payment success and fabricated transaction IDs.
- Rebuilt webhook synchronization around current RevenueCat snapshots and one atomic SQL transaction, including stale-snapshot watermarks, retry safety, transfer reconciliation, payment deduplication and correct local-currency amounts.
- Fixed analysis ownership so caller-supplied IDs cannot read another user's wardrobe. Trip planning now validates dates, uses real provider coordinates and destination timezones, rejects unavailable/out-of-range forecasts, and reports actual outfit totals without inventing weather or calling incomplete clothing sets outfits.
- Removed client writes to authoritative subscriptions; added a migration revoking those writes and protecting subscription/admin/trial profile fields. Removed email-based admin authorization.
- Removed successful fake try-on results when no provider is configured; corrected failure HTTP status codes and an existing trailing syntax error. Removed provider-token values from scan configuration logs.
- Fixed hooks called from calendar, video scan and confirmation event handlers, and conditional hook ordering in clothing detail.
- Aligned the daily usage cache with server UTC boundaries and rejected invalid consumption amounts locally.
- Required athletic evidence during workout candidate selection, matching the final judge.
- Replaced obsolete empty bundled-catalog assumptions in offline tests with explicit test fixtures. No bundled production catalog workaround was restored.
- Fixed clean-install SQL failures: legacy wear-log column assumptions, incorrect hot-path table/column indexes, missing UUID helper and incorrect body-profile timestamp trigger.
- Routed disallowed debug logs through the existing gated/redacting logger and cleared lint errors.
- Added regression tests, load tooling, local SQL verification and CI jobs for migration/security/concurrent quota checks. CI configuration is prepared; a remote CI run has not been observed.

## Release blockers and required evidence

1. **Review and deploy the remaining prepared changes.** Production has the verified security/RPC changes above; migrations `027`/`028` and the updated Edge/mobile code are not deployed. Resolve hosted schema drift, including the missing subscription `auto_renew` column, before deploying functions that use the new admission and atomic subscription-sync RPCs. Configure the dedicated RevenueCat webhook secret, API credential and environment; deploy `sync-subscription` with the matching client. See `docs/SUBSCRIPTION_SETUP.md`. Older clients may log rejected entitlement writes; validate verified webhook activation and post-purchase UI on a real device.
2. **Reconcile migration history before any full hosted push.** The ledger and live schema were inspected. The ledger now contains `001`–`015`, `026`, `029`, and the timestamped server-RPC migration; later historical changes were manually applied. The repository contains two `022` versions and an unnumbered admin migration. Do not blindly reset or push production migrations.
3. **Measure hosted end-to-end capacity.** Use ordinary staging users, representative ownership data and production-equivalent settings. Run baseline, peak, longer soak and burst tests. Observe database connections/locks/query plans, Edge errors/timeouts, Auth rate, storage throughput and image delivery. Staging must exercise the deployed fixes, not just local SQL.
4. **Measure paid-provider and upload flows.** Test actual scan/classification, image/video limits, outfit generation, chat and try-on with provider quotas and representative multilingual/wardrobe inputs. Test malformed and unavailable-provider responses. No provider throughput or universal AI-output-accuracy claim is supported by this audit.
   The replacement key works and hosted `generate-outfits` v74 uses Gemini 3.5 Flash Lite, with one successful production flow. Earlier Flash requests reproduced Google's explicit high-demand 503. Provider throughput, sustained reliability and budget are not certified; the user has deferred this investigation. No new paid-provider plan or recurring spending commitment was made.
5. **Complete native release coverage.** Authentication and recovery, onboarding, scan/edit/delete, save/unsave and persistence, calendar, purchases/restoration, logout/account deletion, deep links, offline recovery, accessibility and supported languages need release-build iOS/Android coverage. Only the recorded native navigation subset was run here.
6. **Review remaining warnings and operations.** Review stale hook dependencies and remaining warnings. Schedule the service-only usage-counter cleanup function and verify operational alerts, provider quotas, backups and incident visibility. The cleanup function is supplied but no hosted schedule was created. Review historical webhook acknowledgements: the old handler could mark failed events processed, and historical repairs cannot be inferred safely from that ledger alone.

The service-role credential shared in chat was not copied into code or evidence. Rotate it through the project owner's normal credential process.

## Evidence

Raw summaries and results are in `docs/reports/readiness-evidence/`. SQL fixtures and assertions are in `scripts/validation/`; the fixed-local-database replay tools connect only to the isolated `/tmp` socket on port 55439. The fresh-database verifier creates a new database and never drops an existing database. Synthetic datasets remain in the local test cluster for inspection.

## Continued audit evidence

The continuation added ownership, trip weather, subscription synchronization and lifecycle regression tests. A new clean database replay passed migrations through `029`, security and webhook lifecycle assertions, and both 800-attempt concurrency scenarios. The updated pending-payment screen exports in the iOS bundle and has TypeScript/unit coverage but has not been validated against a real store purchase on a release device.

Weather response handling was checked against the provider's [current weather schema](https://openweathermap.org/api/current) and [forecast schema](https://openweathermap.org/api/forecast5). Subscription event handling was checked against RevenueCat's [event fields](https://www.revenuecat.com/docs/integrations/webhooks/event-types-and-fields), [webhook guidance](https://www.revenuecat.com/docs/integrations/webhooks), and [Customer Info schema](https://www.revenuecat.com/docs/api-v1/customer-info-model).

### Gemini 3.5 Flash production routing — 2026-10-15 20:18 Tashkent

User selected the exact `gemini-3.5-flash` model. Model listing and a minimal direct generation returned HTTP 200. Production `generate-outfits` v72 now routes all existing tier paths to this model; NVIDIA is disabled for this function, and the retired Gemini 2.0 fallback was removed. JWT verification remains enabled. The key is sent in the request header. Local canonical source uses the same model and passes Deno type checking.

Two isolated production trial-account generation tests both received provider HTTP 503. The existing hosted local builder returned HTTP 200 with `source: local`, so both Gemini acceptance checks FAILED. Both temporary accounts were deleted. Evidence: `production-gemini-35-flash-first.json` and `production-gemini-probe.json`. This is not an end-to-end Gemini success or proof of 10,000 DAU readiness. Other AI endpoints have not been switched in this deployment.

### Google Cloud Console inspection — 2026-10-15 20:23 Tashkent

Inspected the signed-in Firefox Google Cloud Console for project `aiwardrobe-493213`. Gemini API (`generativelanguage.googleapis.com`) is Enabled. Metrics for the displayed 30-day window showed 13 GenerateContent requests with 61.54% errors (mixed historical errors; not a standalone 503 root-cause diagnosis). Quota page showed zero quota categories above 90% current usage or seven-day peak. Filtered `gemini-3.5-flash` quota rows included free limits 5 requests/minute and 20/day, paid tier 1 limits 1,000/minute and 10,000/day, and higher-tier limits. The console lists multiple tiers; inspection did NOT establish the project's active Gemini billing tier. Do not interpret listed paid quotas as proof of entitlement. No billing, access, credential, or quota settings were modified. Exact cause of production provider 503 remains unconfirmed; there is no observed quota-exhaustion evidence in this console view.

### Confirmed Gemini 503 explanation — 2026-10-15 20:25 Tashkent

Direct controlled requests using the same authorized key and exact `gemini-3.5-flash` model produced both HTTP 200 and HTTP 503. A plain outfit JSON prompt succeeded, and the production JSON settings (`responseMimeType: application/json`, temperature 0.95, maxOutputTokens 3000) also succeeded. A subsequent request returned `UNAVAILABLE` with Google's explicit message: "This model is currently experiencing high demand. Spikes in demand are usually temporary. Please try again later." Evidence: `gemini-35-config-diagnostic.json`. This establishes intermittent provider overload for the reproduced 503, not a permanent key or JSON-mode incompatibility. It does not identify Google's internal capacity mechanism or establish that every historical 503 had the same internal cause.

Hosted v73 adds redacted transient-error message logging and prompt character counts, without logging prompts or keys. Another isolated production request still received provider 503 and used the existing local builder; temporary account deleted. Supabase log retrieval failed twice with backend errors, so the exact production response message could not be retrieved in this investigation. Existing generation calls pass `retries = 0`, despite a retry-capable helper, so one transient 503 immediately ends that provider attempt. Current Google troubleshooting guidance recommends bounded exponential backoff and jitter for transient 429/5xx failures. No retry behavior change was deployed as part of this diagnostic.

### Flash Lite switch and verified hosted request — 2026-10-15 20:30 Tashkent

User requested an alternative to overloaded Gemini 3.5 Flash. The initial deployment was rejected by automatic approval review because only a small direct prompt had been tested. Before retrying deployment, executed the hosted handler in Node against real production Auth, Database, catalog and Gemini Flash Lite, isolated to a temporary trial account. All three acceptance checks passed, one complete three-item outfit passed the style validator, and the account was deleted (`gemini-lite-real-services-predeployment.json`). This predeployment test excludes the hosted Edge runtime/gateway.

After that evidence, deployment was approved: hosted `generate-outfits` v74, JWT verification enabled, bundle SHA256 `7a00a54912652f29e5036ac6c554159dc13ec2072e4271be8a882295d2ff2b40`. Only model routing changed from v73 to `gemini-3.5-flash-lite`. The canonical local generator uses this model and passes Deno type checking. An actual hosted production request then passed all three acceptance checks: HTTP 200, source `ai`, provider `gemini:gemini-3.5-flash-lite`, one outfit with three items and images, 12,991ms. Temporary account deletion confirmed HTTP 200 (`production-gemini-probe.json`). One hosted success is not a provider capacity test or a downtime guarantee. Other AI functions were not switched by this deployment.

### Non-provider audit: account deletion and OAuth — 2026-10-15 21:25 Tashkent

Provider capacity and credit-account investigation deferred at the user's request. Baseline mobile TypeScript and 982 tests in 74 suites passed before this continuation.

A controlled production deletion test demonstrated a privacy defect: hosted `delete-account` v10 returned success and removed the identity/profile while a nested `user_uploads` object and a user-owned flat `AIWARDROBE` object remained accessible. The harness removed those explicitly created leftovers and the control account. No existing users or files were modified. Before evidence: `production-account-deletion-before.json`.

Deployed the `account_deletion_storage` migration and `delete-account` v11 (JWT verification enabled; bundle SHA256 `82b3ed888a2d1040ae986ccfcdaa562712d8410c7a51f476e6cdc4c35eba6375`). A service-role-only, security-invoker RPC enumerates the user's owned files across buckets plus unowned files under their UUID folder, in batches of at most 500. Objects are removed through the Storage API, not SQL. The function checks cleanup errors and remaining objects before deleting the Auth user; existing foreign keys then cascade relational data in that transaction. Fixed the legacy referral foreign key so referral rows cannot block account deletion. Only POST can trigger deletion. Bounded cleanup reports a retriable failure instead of falsely claiming completion for accounts whose objects cannot be cleared in one invocation.

The deployed function passed 14 production checks: nested/root/cross-bucket uploads removed, Auth/profile/wardrobe removed, other-user files/account preserved despite a caller-supplied other-user ID, GET rejected, anonymous deletion denied, storage enumeration denied to both client roles, and the deleted identity's token rejected by Auth. All disposable fixture files/accounts were cleaned up. After evidence: `production-account-deletion.json`. Earlier historical orphaned files were not deleted; original ownership cannot safely be inferred after identity deletion.

Prepared mobile fixes: auth callback URLs containing credentials are no longer logged; OAuth failures are read from query or fragment and decoded exactly once, preventing literal percent signs from throwing before the UI can display the error; deletion requires an explicit `success: true` before clearing local account state. These mobile changes are not a released native binary. Added regression coverage for storage errors, bounded cleanup, identity isolation, non-POST calls, callback error decoding and missing deletion confirmation.

A fresh replay exposed migration `030` assuming two legacy hosted login handlers existed. Hardening now runs conditionally when those handlers are present. All 31 numbered migrations then installed successfully in a new isolated PostgreSQL database. Storage ownership/cascade assertions passed separately, including preservation of another user's owned file placed under the first user's folder. The local test server was stopped afterward.

Final validation: 991 tests / 75 suites pass; mobile TypeScript passes; revised Edge function passes pinned-lock Deno checking; whitespace diff check passes. Security advisors report no ERROR-level notices after deployment; existing warnings remain: 10 mutable-search-path functions, 5 anonymous and 8 authenticated callable security-definer-function findings, and leaked-password protection disabled. Remediation: [Supabase database linter](https://supabase.com/docs/guides/database/database-linter) and [password security](https://supabase.com/docs/guides/auth/password-security). These warnings were not represented as fixed by this change.

### Non-provider audit: private profile photos — 2026-10-15

Production avatar upload failed with HTTP 400 because the Profile screen targeted a missing `avatars` bucket. The client also swallowed upload failures and used a Blob upload path unsuitable for React Native. Before evidence: `production-avatar-before.json`.

Deployed `private_profile_avatar_storage` (local migration `20261015162830_profile_avatar_storage.sql`): private avatars bucket, 5 MiB maximum, image MIME allowlist, and owner-folder SELECT/INSERT/UPDATE/DELETE policies. Automatic approval review rejected the initial public-bucket proposal because profile-photo disclosure was not specifically authorized. That proposal was not deployed; the private alternative passed review and verification.

Prepared mobile code uploads an ArrayBuffer, normalizes JPEG MIME, validates size/type, reports upload failures, and requires a returned profile row before closing the editor. Failed profile saves remove their newly uploaded file. The profile stores an authenticated Storage URL without embedding tokens. Image requests attach the current session JWT only for this project's authenticated avatar endpoint, use memory caching, and refresh headers when the session changes. These client changes still require a native release.

All 17 production avatar assertions passed: owner upload/download/replace, persisted profile URL, no public read, no other-user read/list/write/delete, MIME and oversize rejection, anonymous rejection, and avatar deletion with account deletion while preserving the control account. Both temporary HTTP fixture accounts and files were removed. Evidence: `production-avatar.json`. A preceding attempt experienced a transport failure on the oversized-upload request after twelve checks; its cleanup ran, and the complete repeat passed. This is functional verification, not sustained upload capacity certification.

Seven avatar regression tests pass; full Jest passes 998 tests in 76 suites; mobile TypeScript passes. Targeted lint has zero errors and eight warnings. All 32 migrations replay on a fresh isolated PostgreSQL database, including avatar ownership/RLS assertions. Post-deployment security advisors report no ERROR-level findings; prior warnings remain.

Native verification uses a separate iOS 26.5 simulator and a disposable ordinary production account, preserving the user's existing signed-in simulator. First-launch UI verification is currently blocked because macOS is locked; a language-screen assertion also failed, so native avatar rendering and photo-picker/save interaction are not claimed as passed. The disposable native fixture account and avatar were deleted successfully after refreshing its expired session (initial 401, refresh 200, deletion 200). Evidence: `native-avatar-status.json`. ### Shared-device logout cleanup — 2026-10-15

Source audit found that `outfitLogs`, legacy `@outfit_logs`, `@ai_chat_history`, `@selected_style`, `savedInspo`, recency keys and the notification scheduling marker were absent from the per-user cleanup registry. Calendar/Profile/AIOutfitmaker read the shared calendar key, and AIAssistant read the shared chat key. Saved shop likes also remained in their Zustand singleton after logout. These omissions could expose the prior account's local data on a shared device.

Registered the missing keys and dynamic recency prefix for logout/account-deletion cleanup. Added saved-shop memory reset with a hydration revision so an old asynchronous read cannot refill the store after reset. Three regressions exercise actual AsyncStorage cleanup, preserve device language, reject a delayed old-account hydration and load fresh current-account likes. This addresses the identified omissions; it is not proof that every asynchronous producer or persistence path is race-free.

Full validation passes 1,001 tests / 77 suites and mobile TypeScript; targeted lint reports zero errors and eleven warnings (lazy require pattern). Evidence: `shared-device-cleanup.json`. This is a prepared mobile change, not a published native release or device-level account-switch certification. Production data/settings were unchanged in this continuation. Provider investigation remains deferred; native verification still requires unlocking the Mac.

### Calendar write integrity — 2026-10-15

Active Calendar and AIOutfitmaker paths previously replaced the whole `outfitLogs` JSON from independent snapshots. A delayed save/delete could discard another date's edits. They now share a serialized read/modify/write service reading the latest disk snapshot for each mutation; Profile trip reads use that service too. A failed operation rejects without poisoning subsequent saves, and corrupt top-level storage is reported rather than silently discarded. Account revision checks reject queued or prepared operations crossing logout; cleanup drains an already-running write before removing its key.

Calendar success feedback now occurs after storage succeeds. Failures show translated English/Russian/Uzbek messages, preserve the draft and keep the modal open. The generator's asynchronous replacement callback catches save failures, and a date newly occupied during an initial save is not silently replaced without confirmation.

Six regressions cover concurrent date saves/deletion, failed-write recovery, corruption preservation, queued account transition, a prepared pre-logout operation and draft/error/success-feedback behavior. Full Jest passes 1,007 tests / 78 suites; mobile TypeScript passes; whitespace checks pass. Targeted lint on the touched active screens/services reports zero errors and 213 warnings, including existing screen warnings. These are local persistence checks, not hosted calendar synchronization, native device coverage or a released binary. The separate unused legacy `useOutfitLogs` implementation still uses `@outfit_logs`; it is not asserted to share the active calendar queue. Evidence: `calendar-integrity.json`.

### Calendar civil-date correctness — 2026-10-16

Fixed calendar/grid future-day comparisons to compare canonical local date keys rather than UTC-parsed midnight timestamps. The outfit-maker date parameter now rejects malformed or impossible dates instead of letting JavaScript normalize them into a different day. Day arrows use local `setDate` arithmetic rather than adding/subtracting 86,400,000 milliseconds, preventing repeated/skipped calendar dates across daylight-saving changes. The log factory validates dates; legacy monthly statistics compare date keys instead of converting them to UTC instants.

Twelve regressions include independent Node processes under America/Los_Angeles, Europe/Berlin, Asia/Tashkent and Pacific/Kiritimati. They verify spring/fall navigation for US and European transition dates, year rollover, exact date parsing, low-year handling, invalid dates and valid leap days. The initial timezone harness failed before exercising code due to a duplicate declaration; this was corrected before the passing measurements. Full Jest passes 1,019 tests / 79 suites; TypeScript passes; targeted lint has zero errors and 206 warnings; whitespace checks pass. Evidence: `calendar-date-correctness.json`. Tests cover local date logic, not rendered native interaction, cross-device synchronization or release distribution. No production changes in this continuation.

### Wardrobe synchronization integrity — 2026-10-16

Audited the active wardrobe pending-action path. Sync replaced the whole item list with its starting snapshot, which could discard additions/edits or resurrect removals made during a request. Overlapping sync calls could replay the same batch. Sync and direct fetch responses could also publish after an account transition. The sync slice now shares an in-flight operation per account revision, applies conflict results only to unchanged starting items, preserves newer local changes and newly queued actions, and discards stale account results. Direct fetch responses have matching account guards.

The service previously acknowledged queued deletes and wear-log upserts even when Supabase returned an error. It now retains those actions on returned errors, stops add-item reconciliation when timestamp/conflict reads fail, explicitly filters deletes by owner, and checks account identity/revision before subsequent mutations. A read-only production invalid-column request using the installed Supabase SDK returned `data: null` and error `42703` without throwing, confirming the response contract behind this fix. No production records changed. Evidence: `supabase-error-contract.json`. Error handling was checked against [upsert](https://supabase.com/docs/reference/javascript/upsert) and [delete](https://supabase.com/docs/reference/javascript/delete) documentation; the changelog Markdown fetch was attempted but the browsing tool rejected its content type.

Seven new regressions cover local-edit/add/delete preservation, queued-action retention, single-flight calls, old-account fetch/sync rejection, returned Supabase errors and expected-account checks. Two existing service mocks were corrected to support the newly explicit owner filter; assertions were preserved. Full Jest passes 1,026 tests / 81 suites; mobile TypeScript passes; targeted lint has zero errors and ten warnings; whitespace checks pass. Evidence: `wardrobe-sync-integrity.json`. This is prepared mobile code with controlled mock network responses plus a read-only live SDK contract check, not hosted offline-replay, native release or universal retry/idempotency certification. Separate legacy upload/offline queue services were inspected but not changed in this continuation.

### Wear-history and utilization accuracy — 2026-10-16

Wear history previously counted all logged item identifiers toward closet utilization, including removed or external items, allowing results over 100%. Utilization now counts the intersection with the current closet. Utilization/unworn windows exclude future logs and use an inclusive local-calendar window (today plus the preceding 29 days for a 30-day request). Empty wear input no longer adds a log or queued action, and duplicate/empty item IDs are normalized before recording.

New wear dates use the local day. Store and Profile now share streak calculation, with local-day arithmetic, yesterday grace, duplicate-day deduplication and invalid/future-day exclusion. Existing historical date strings are preserved; their original intended timezone cannot safely be inferred and was not rewritten.

Seven new regressions cover utilization bounds, date windows, invalid/future/duplicate dates, empty input and independent Node timezone checks at a fixed near-midnight instant in Tashkent, Los Angeles and UTC+14. Full Jest passes 1,033 tests / 82 suites; TypeScript passes; targeted lint reports zero errors and five warnings; whitespace checks pass. Evidence: `wear-history-accuracy.json`. These checks cover local statistics and new log construction, not native background/midnight refresh, hosted wear-count reconciliation or a published app. No production mutations were performed in this continuation.

### Clothing-detail save integrity — 2026-10-16

The detail editor previously changed the local wardrobe before server confirmation and left that change visible after a failed save. It now updates the store only after a response confirms the expected item ID; missing confirmation is an error, the editor remains open, and account-revision changes discard stale results. Saving uses one typed payload for server/local updates.

The type selector now recognizes canonical categories (`top`, `bottom`, `accessory`, etc.), offers dresses, and persists a changed canonical category alongside its subtype. A color-only save preserves the original garment subtype instead of replacing `t-shirt` with generic `tops`. Existing sportswear/homewear subtype selections retain the current canonical category. Type labels and save errors use translation keys, with new dress/other/edit labels in English, Russian and Uzbek.

Five rendered React Native component tests with controlled API responses verify failed-save preservation, confirmed category persistence, unchanged subtype, account switch and missing confirmation. Initial component tests needed the selection-haptics mock and clean per-test API mock implementations before passing. Full Jest passes 1,038 tests / 83 suites; TypeScript passes; targeted lint reports zero errors and two warnings; whitespace checks pass. Evidence: `clothing-detail-integrity.json`. Identity and wear-counter fields are not accepted through that payload. Existing account-transition guards remain. Other typed custom-backend routes are not asserted to be repaired by this change.

### Favorite and deletion persistence; insert retry identity — 2026-10-16

Removed the wardrobe store's remaining custom-backend calls for favorite toggles and item deletion. Favorite taps now append explicit desired boolean values to the existing persisted pending-action queue and trigger synchronization. The sync service writes owner-scoped `is_favorite` updates, checks errors and requires a matching returned row. Replays write the same value instead of toggling again. Newly queued user actions receive a follow-up sync after an existing operation finishes; failed old actions alone do not cause a retry spin. Item deletion uses direct owner-filtered Supabase removal, retains the existing offline queue behavior on errors, and rejects stale-account follow-up work.

New inserts now send the optimistic UUID to Supabase. If the response is lost, pending upsert retries use that same row ID, preventing a second garment with a different server-generated ID. Insert confirmation preserves newer local edits (including favorite state) rather than replacing them with the starting snapshot. Account-revision checks prevent late inserts/errors from writing into the next account's store.

Executed actual editor/deletion/sync service sources against two disposable ordinary production accounts. All 12 checks passed: previous six edit/security checks plus favorite true persistence, repeated false idempotency, rejection/non-acknowledgement of another user's favorite edit, preservation against another user's delete, owner deletion, and repeated owner deletion. Both fixture accounts were deleted. Evidence: `production-wardrobe-mutations.json`; harness enabled with `MUTATION_CHECKS=1`. Cross-user deletion intentionally returns success for the caller's already-absent owner-scoped row without revealing another user's record; the owner row remained present. No existing-user data changed.

Five additional unit regressions verify explicit favorite acknowledgements/error retention, rapid-tap payload ordering, stable insert UUID after response loss, account-switch failure isolation and preservation of edits during insert confirmation. The existing sync-race test now also asserts exactly one follow-up request. Full Jest passes 1,046 tests / 85 suites; TypeScript passes; targeted lint reports zero errors and 13 warnings; whitespace checks pass. Evidence: `wardrobe-mutation-queue.json`. ### Wardrobe primary reads and refresh reconciliation — 2026-10-16

Wardrobe fetch/rehydration now uses Supabase directly instead of first waiting on the mismatched custom-backend route. Owner-scoped clothing reads page in batches of 250 with stable `created_at`/`id` ordering, replacing the former 500-row truncation. A failed page returns no partial replacement, preserving the existing cache. The 60-second per-account TTL remains. Pagination over a changing live dataset is not claimed to provide a transactional snapshot.

Refresh reconciles cloud items with pending additions, removals and explicit favorite values and preserves local changes made while the read was in flight. Wear history is refreshed even when a local cache exists; new/pending local logs are retained. Existing wear-history Data API limits are not asserted to provide unlimited history coverage. Account-revision checks also cover the AsyncStorage hydration wait, and an old request's completion cannot clear a newer request's in-flight handle.

### Saved-outfit confirmation and cloud unsave — 2026-10-16

The outfit maker previously marked its local outfit saved and showed success even if cloud insertion failed, and cloud insertion used a different ID from the local outfit. It now upserts the same stable local ID through `saveCloudOutfit`, requires a matching returned row, and only then marks the outfit saved locally. Missing sessions, invalid dates, returned errors and account transitions reject the save. Repeated saves use the same record. New standalone dates use the local calendar day. A connected calendar-link failure now reports an error instead of claiming successful linkage; closet/cloud and local calendar writes are still separate operations, not an atomic transaction.

Unsave now awaits owner-scoped cloud deletion before changing the local saved flag; failure leaves the local save intact. The Closet caller catches/report errors. Profile fetch no longer skips cloud outfits just because local saves exist, accepts stored `imageUrl` as well as `image`, and discards stale account responses. Existing visibility metadata/RLS access is unchanged by deployment of database policies (no policies were changed).

Executed actual saved-outfit service source using two disposable ordinary production accounts. Eight saved-outfit assertions passed: persisted row/items, one row after repeated save, other-user read/overwrite protection, owner row preserved against cross-user delete, owner unsave removal, repeated-unsave idempotency and anonymous rejection. Together with the harness's six clothing-editor assertions this run passed 14/14; both accounts and their rows were removed. Evidence: `production-saved-outfits.json`; actual-source harness uses `OUTFIT_CHECKS=1` and a substituted auth-event revision reader.

Six unit/store regressions cover stable IDs/owner mapping, error/missing confirmation, owner-filtered unsave, invalid date/session, failed-unsave local preservation and account-switch completion isolation. Full Jest passes 1,058 tests / 88 suites; TypeScript passes; targeted lint reports zero errors and 227 warnings across the large touched screens; whitespace checks pass. Older saves with different local/cloud IDs were not automatically reconciled or deleted; duplicate historical rows and legacy unsave linkage remain unverified. ### Debounced persistence and current platform exports — 2026-10-16

The wardrobe storage adapter left superseded write promises unresolved and did not reject callers when AsyncStorage failed. The replacement settles every coalesced caller, propagates errors to awaited calls while handling background rejections, orders writes/removals per key, and cancels pending writes before deletion. Reads resolving after a removal cannot return that key's previous value. Logout/account deletion invokes ordered wardrobe cleanup before the broader key removal. AppState inactive/background transitions explicitly flush pending wardrobe writes; native background execution/OS termination timing remains unverified.

An initial adapter implementation introduced an extra read microtask and caused two existing wardrobe tests to fail: hydration could select an intermediate optimistic snapshot and later undo a second add or removal. Corrected the read to capture its disk/pending snapshot at invocation and added a dedicated regression. All seven new adapter regressions and the existing store/cleanup tests pass, including coalescing, cancellation, in-flight write ordering, failure recovery, flush/read visibility and stale-read protection. Existing cleanup coverage now asserts ordered wardrobe cleanup precedes bulk removal.

### Profile-response and login-follow-up isolation — 2026-10-16

The continuation was interrupted after inspection/commentary, before implementation. Re-read authoritative source on resume; no prior mutation needed recovery. A process-list query was unavailable in the sandbox, but that interrupted inspection had started no persistent commands.

`fetchUser` could publish an old account's profile, clear a new account's profile after a late exception, or replace a known profile with incomplete session metadata after a transient SDK error. The login-success pipeline now clears a previous account's displayed profile when publishing a different session, emits the login identity before fetching that profile, and guards analytics, identity setup, trial initialization and wardrobe follow-up boundaries after async waits. Restored-session initialization emits its identity before launching profile enrichment, so its own login event does not invalidate that enrichment. This does not claim cancellation of an identity request that was legitimately started before a later account switch, nor full coverage of every external SDK auth-event cleanup path.

### Deleted-token Storage gap closed — 2026-10-16

A new live test demonstrated that an already deleted disposable account's JWT still uploaded a new PNG into both `avatars` and `user_uploads` (HTTP 200). The prior Auth-user probe correctly returned 403, but that did not prove Storage access was closed. The attempted garment insert was rejected (409). All probe files were removed through the Storage API, including the files created after deletion; the other account was preserved until fixture cleanup. Before evidence: `production-deleted-token-writes-before.json`.

This matches the documented JWT expiry window after Auth deletion. Reviewed current [user-deletion guidance](https://supabase.com/docs/guides/auth/managing-user-data), [session guidance](https://supabase.com/docs/guides/auth/sessions) and changelog index before implementation. The current CBC-TLS and PostgreSQL minor-release notices do not change the session-row validation mechanism used here; no project TLS/database upgrade was performed.

Created the migration filename with the cached Supabase CLI, then deployed `storage_live_session_guard` from `20261016041316_storage_live_session_guard.sql`. It adds one restrictive authenticated-role policy to `storage.objects`, requiring a matching still-existing `auth.sessions` row in addition to all prior bucket/owner rules. A stable, search-path-hardened SECURITY DEFINER boolean helper in the previously absent `app_security` schema checks the signed session ID and owner using the session primary key. Missing/malformed/other-user session claims return false. Clients receive no direct Auth-table grants; helper execution is restricted and the schema is outside the exposed Data API. Anonymous public reads and service-role cleanup retain their prior paths.

After deployment, all 17 deletion/stale-token checks passed, including rejection of both formerly successful uploads. All 17 existing avatar lifecycle/isolation checks also passed for ordinary valid users. Four disposable accounts across the two after-deployment harnesses and all their files were cleaned. A non-mutating anonymous helper-schema API request returned 406/PGRST106, confirming the helper schema is not exposed. Evidence: `production-deleted-token-writes.json`, `production-avatar.json`, `storage-helper-api-boundary.json`. The deployed policy was independently confirmed RESTRICTIVE / authenticated / ALL via catalog query.

The isolated local test server initially started with default localhost port settings; authoritative status inspection identified that configuration and it was corrected to the intended 55440 private Unix socket with TCP disabled. A test fixture also needed email values for the existing signup trigger; it was corrected before successful assertions. Fresh replay passed all 33 migrations plus active/revoked/missing/malformed/mismatched session assertions, avatar ownership and service cleanup. ### Bounded profile requests — 2026-10-16

Profile reads had no application deadline. Added a portable `withRequestDeadline` helper and applied a 15-second bound to `fetchUser`'s profile query, passing its AbortSignal into the Supabase query builder. The caller settles even if an operation ignores cancellation; late results cannot replace that timeout outcome. Successful requests and synchronous failures clear their timer. On timeout, existing profile/account guards preserve a known current profile and retain an error rather than inventing a successful refresh.

Verified against the installed SDK source and current [AbortSignal reference](https://supabase.com/docs/reference/javascript/using-modifiers-abortsignal), discovered through the Supabase documentation connector after an obsolete short URL failed. Installed React Native source includes the AbortController/AbortSignal polyfills used by this implementation. A real installed Supabase client with a controlled, non-network transport demonstrated abort propagation. The initial transport mock used a generic error and therefore exercised SDK retry behavior; corrected it to the AbortError emitted by a cancelled fetch. Profile restore coverage now waits for the observable enriched state instead of assuming one microtask, and its auth-listener mock resolves at invocation time.

Six new regressions cover hung operations, successful timer cleanup, late completion, synchronous failure, SDK cancellation forwarding and a stalled profile read preserving the known profile after 15 seconds. Full Jest passes 1,082 tests / 92 suites; TypeScript passes; targeted lint reports zero errors and 19 warnings; whitespace checks pass. Re-exported current iOS and Android Hermes bundles; exact artifacts/hashes and installed SDK version are in `profile-request-deadline.json`, under separate `/tmp/aiwardrobe-readiness-deadline-{ios,android}-20261016` directories.

These are controlled timer/transport and store checks, not native flaky-network runtime evidence. The bound applies to the profile query, not every initialization, subscription, Auth or data request, and not the entire login pipeline. No production mutations or provider calls occurred in this continuation. Native release validation, deferred provider capacity and the overall readiness objective remain incomplete.

### SDK auth-event memory isolation — 2026-10-16

Same-account token refresh preserves profile, memory and revision. No awaited SDK call was introduced inside the auth callback, consistent with the current [auth-event reference](https://supabase.com/docs/reference/javascript/auth-onauthstatechange). ### Daily usage response/cache integrity — 2026-10-16

Added auth-revision and UTC-day guards to daily usage reads/responses so old-account results cannot populate a new account, and yesterday's delayed result cannot consume today's displayed allowance. The existing six-second RPC race now uses the shared deadline helper, which clears its timer when the request settles.

### Complete wear-history reads — 2026-10-16

`fetchWearLogsFromServer` used one unpaginated `select('*')`, leaving older history vulnerable to the Data API response cap and downstream statistics to incomplete input. It now reads explicit required columns in owner-filtered 250-row pages, with deterministic descending date/id ordering. Session/account revision is checked after session acquisition and every page; any error, absent result or account change returns null so the existing rehydrate logic retains cached history rather than replacing it with partial data.

Verified against the current [range reference](https://supabase.com/docs/reference/javascript/using-modifiers-range): offsets are inclusive and require ordering. Three new controlled tests cover a complete 1,001-entry history over five pages, a later-page failure and a stale account response. All 1,098 Jest tests / 93 suites pass; TypeScript, targeted lint (zero errors/warnings) and whitespace checks pass. Evidence: `wear-history-pagination.json`.

No hosted long-history or native rendering test is claimed. Offset pages are separate queries and do not provide snapshot consistency during concurrent writes; that remains a material limitation. No production mutation, provider call or deployment occurred. Overall 10,000-DAU readiness remains uncertified.

### Startup session ownership and observer cleanup — 2026-10-16

Added five regressions that failed before the fix: late old-session restore overriding a completed login, stale refresh-token failure invoking sign-out for a newer session, overlapping initialization replacing the latest result/registering duplicate observers, pending reads registering observers after navigator disposal, and queued unsubscribed callbacks clearing a newer account.

### Wear-counter durability deployed and verified — 2026-10-16

A rollback-only production probe confirmed that one inserted wear log left its garment count at zero. Deployed migration 20261016141704_wear_log_counters through MCP after local validation. Owner-scoped, invoker triggers now adjust garment counts atomically with log inserts, membership changes and deletion; repeat upserts do not double-count. Garment edits from authenticated clients retain server statistics instead of overwriting them with stale cached values. Functions use an empty search_path and revoked PUBLIC execution. Canonical garment lock order covers opposite outfit ordering in the app's single-log transaction path.

The first migration request returned expired requestState; a catalog check confirmed it had not applied before the successful retry. Production assertions using authenticated-role mutations passed for increment, duplicate/malformed IDs, ownership, retries, direct overwrite protection, membership changes, timestamp correction and deletion. All randomized fixtures were rolled back; follow-up queries found zero wear logs, zero test-domain users and both expected triggers. Pre-deployment production had zero wear logs and zero nonzero/timestamp statistics, so no historical backfill was performed. Existing security advisor findings remained unchanged.

Locally, 12 concurrent workers inserted 120 logs containing the same two garments in alternating order: counts were exactly 120 each. Concurrent deletion returned counts and timestamps to zero. All 34 migrations replayed into a fresh database and the regression SQL passed; CI now runs that regression. The client now starts sync immediately after queueing a wear log, preserving the queued operation on failure. An isolated slice fixture initially lacked syncToServer; updated its mock to the actual store contract. All 1,111 Jest tests / 95 suites, TypeScript and whitespace checks pass; targeted lint has zero errors/two existing warnings.

Evidence: `wear-counter-durability.json`; regression scripts: `scripts/validation/wear-log-counters.sql` and `wear-counter-concurrency.py`. Correction to an earlier progress update: both the local baseline and production item_ids columns are JSONB; conversion support for legacy UUID arrays is additional, not an end-to-end tested second schema. Hosted concurrent write capacity, arbitrary multi-log transaction deadlock behavior and latest-source native builds remain unverified. AI remains deferred; overall 10,000-DAU readiness is not certified.

### Pending offline mutations bypass refresh cache — 2026-10-16

Reproduced a refresh ordering bug: after a successful cloud refresh, pending offline changes were skipped by the 60-second TTL guard before syncToServer was reached. The failing regression observed zero sync attempts where one was expected. The TTL guard now applies only when the pending queue is empty. A second regression confirms the no-pending-work path still skips redundant cloud reads.

All 1,113 Jest tests / 95 suites and TypeScript pass. Targeted lint has zero errors/four existing warnings; whitespace passes. Evidence: `pending-sync-cache-bypass.json`. This is a source fix awaiting native rebuild/release; no production mutations or provider calls occurred. It does not introduce a connectivity listener or autonomous retry timer, and real-device offline recovery remains to be verified. Overall readiness remains uncertified.

### Preserve pending wear statistics during reconciliation — 2026-10-16

A failing regression showed a refresh replacing an optimistic garment count of 3/latest date October 16 with cloud count 2/October 15 while the unsynced wear log remained queued. Reconciliation now retains the greater local/cloud count and latest valid timestamp only for garments referenced by pending wear logs. It does not add another increment, so a committed upload whose acknowledgement was lost does not double-count. Other garment fields still refresh; after acknowledgement the normal cloud values apply. Tests also retain higher/newer cloud statistics and avoid resurrecting deleted garments. These values remain optimistic until synchronization succeeds; exact cross-device totals cannot be established offline.

All 1,114 Jest tests / 95 suites, TypeScript, targeted lint (zero warnings/errors) and whitespace pass. Source changes still require native rebuild/release. Evidence: `pending-wear-statistics.json`. No production mutations or provider calls occurred.

The desktop was accessible through CUA again, revealing the original user iOS simulator; no interaction was made with that account. Booted the dedicated Android readiness AVD successfully (sys.boot_completed=1). Its window was absent from CUA app inventory and selecting the executable path returned Invalid app. No alternative UI automation path or false runtime pass was used. Stopped only the dedicated emulator; Android visual launch remains unverified, now due to surface availability rather than the earlier desktop lock. Overall readiness remains uncertified.

### Favorite retries retain the latest user choice — 2026-10-16

Review of the mutation queue found that processing every favorite tap independently could acknowledge a newer successful value while retaining an older failed value for retry, eventually undoing the latest choice. Valid favorite actions are now grouped by garment; only the final queued value is sent, and all superseded entries are acknowledged only after the final write returns the matching row. Failure preserves the whole group and retries send the latest value again. Owner filters and strict confirmation remain in place.

The latest-value regression failed before the change (two writes instead of one). Added failure/retry coverage verifies zero acknowledgements after failure, both entries acknowledged after success, and the latest false value sent on both attempts. All 1,116 Jest tests / 95 suites, TypeScript, targeted lint (zero warnings/errors) and whitespace pass. Evidence: `favorite-retry-ordering.json`. No production mutations/provider calls; native rebuild/release and actual flaky-network verification remain required. Queued/in-flight creation versus deletion is a separate outstanding concern identified by source review, not yet claimed fixed or reproduced live. Overall readiness remains uncertified.

### Garment deletion supersedes old uploads — 2026-10-16

Reproduced a queue failure: successful later deletion acknowledged only itself while old upload/favorite entries remained eligible for retry. The queue now groups mutations superseded by a later deletion with a linear reverse scan, does not send those obsolete writes, and acknowledges the group only after deletion succeeds. Failure retains the group for retry.

removeItem now records deletion together with local removal and uses the existing serialized sync pipeline instead of sending a separate unqueued delete. Direct addItem confirmation/error checks whether the optimistic garment was removed in the meantime; if so it queues an idempotent removal rather than publishing/requeueing creation. A pre-insert check also avoids starting creation if removal happened during session lookup. Regression cases cover both late insert success and a lost/error response after the first deletion was already acknowledged, plus account-switch isolation.

All 1,119 Jest tests / 95 suites, TypeScript and whitespace pass; targeted lint zero errors/three existing warnings. Evidence: `garment-deletion-ordering.json`. Source only, no production mutations/provider calls. These tests cover an app that remains running until requests settle. Process death after server insertion but before compensating removal is persisted remains unproven; a server tombstone/durable creation protocol would be required to claim that stronger guarantee. Native races, latest-source builds and hosted write capacity remain outstanding; overall readiness remains uncertified.

### Cursor pagination avoids concurrent-deletion gaps — 2026-10-16

Both wardrobe and wear-log readers failed a regression in which another device deletes a first-page row before the second page loads: offset movement skipped an unseen record. Replaced offset traversal with descending immutable-ID cursors (lt plus a 250-row limit), then chronological sorting of the assembled results. Checked current Supabase lt documentation and installed SDK implementations before editing. Both concurrent-deletion cases now pass. Additional checks preserve 501-item/1,001-log completeness, chronological display, later-page failure/account-change cache safety and termination on a repeated/non-advancing cursor.

All 1,124 Jest tests / 95 suites, TypeScript, targeted lint (zero warnings/errors) and whitespace pass. Evidence: `wardrobe-keyset-pagination.json`. This is not snapshot isolation: concurrent inserts above an already crossed cursor may wait for the next refresh, and later row updates can appear across requests. Composite owner/id index/query-plan verification is the next performance concern; no hosted capacity claim is added. No production writes/provider calls occurred. This source edit postdates the newly built Android APK, so a subsequent rebuild is needed. Overall readiness remains uncertified.

### Owner/ID cursor indexes measured and deployed — 2026-10-16

Production lacked composite owner/ID indexes for the new cursor reads. Reviewed current query-optimization documentation and measured authenticated-RLS plans on 10,000 local synthetic owners, 510,000 garments, 110,000 wear logs and 50,000 outfits. The largest owner has 10,050 garments/10,010 logs. Before indexing, garment first/later-page queries used the global primary key, filtering 12,981/11,844 other rows and taking 7.982/7.222ms. Wear-log queries filtered 2,785/2,550 rows and took 1.351/1.247ms.

Migration 20261016152500 adds owner/id-desc indexes with 3s lock and 30s statement deadlines. After local application, all four plans use the intended indexes and filter zero other-owner rows: garment first/later 0.321/0.303ms, logs 0.168/0.138ms. These are individual local runs with warm-cache effects, not controlled latency distributions. Deployed through MCP to the observed small production tables (109 garments/zero logs); catalog confirms both indexes valid/ready and definitions correct. Existing security-advisor counts are unchanged. Initial migration-new CLI attempt hit sandbox telemetry EPERM; normal reviewed escalation generated the file successfully.

A 30-second local pgbench workload runs both later-page reads per transaction with authenticated owner RLS and random owner selection from the 10,000-user population. With 40 clients/four threads and 200 offered TPS: 6,117 transactions, zero failures, 204.065 achieved TPS, mean latency 3.621ms, standard deviation 2.322ms, mean/max schedule lag 1.921/20.739ms. Installed pgbench emits debug logging; overhead is included. This short synthetic database check is not production HTTP, real Auth verification, write/upload/AI capacity or certification of 10,000 simultaneous users.

All 35 migrations and wear-counter assertions replayed successfully into a fresh database. Evidence: `cursor-query-plans-before.json`, `cursor-query-plans-after.json`, `cursor-capacity-summary.txt`, `cursor-index-verification.json`. ### Hosted wear writes failed; Auth deletion regression fixed — 2026-10-16

Built a bounded write harness and validated it against successful paired-ID writes, 429 responses, wrong-owner confirmations, invalid JSON, transport errors, cancellation and dropped scheduling. ### Media storage gap found; photo data-URL corruption fixed — 2026-10-17

Tracing the active WardrobeVideoScreen/useVideoAnalysis and ClothingDetailEditor paths shows the photo string passed directly into addItem, then clothing_items.image_url. The wardrobe reader selects that full field. No object-storage step exists on this client save path. The separate uploadQueueProcessor has no call sites and was not treated as an active recovery path.

Read-only production aggregates (without downloading photo content or exposing user IDs/paths) show 109 garments across 30 owners: 18 data-URL images total 15,885,701 bytes, two file/content device-local references and 87 HTTP images. Total image_url bytes are 15,896,398; maximum single field 4,047,638. Maximum owner image payload 5,281,000 bytes; owner-payload p95 3,600,614.2 bytes. The maximum garment count among owners is eight (a separate aggregate, not necessarily the same owner as the largest payload). thumbnail_url totals zero bytes. Device-local references are not portable to another device and can become unavailable after cache eviction. Inline images inflate relational storage, full-wardrobe reads and persisted JSON. Existing short-URL capacity fixtures do not cover this workload; no broader media/read-capacity certification is inferred from their pass.

Separately, two hook regressions reproduced original-photo corruption when analysis is unavailable or throws: an already complete PNG data URL became data:image/jpeg;base64,data:image/png;base64,... . Exported/reused the existing original-image formatter in those paths; both now preserve the input exactly. This fixes image formatting only, not the existing provider fallback policy or media storage architecture. No provider calls/configuration changes or production writes occurred.

All 1,129 Jest tests / 96 suites, TypeScript and whitespace pass. Targeted lint has zero errors/six existing warnings. Evidence: `production-media-audit.json`. A complete media fix still needs durable private object storage, correct client display/provider access, offline behavior, migration of recoverable inline images and appropriate treatment of unavailable local files. These requirements are not replaced by shrinking images in database strings or accepting broken references. Latest native artifacts predate the source changes. Overall readiness remains uncertified.

### Private media foundation deployed; cutover remains open — 2026-10-17

Implemented wardrobeMedia upload/access primitives and deployed migration 20261017001357. The new wardrobe-images bucket is private, limited to 10MiB and accepted image MIME types, with owner-folder SELECT/INSERT/UPDATE/DELETE policies. The existing restrictive live-session policy also applies. No existing photo row was migrated and no active garment-save/render path was switched, so the inline/local-reference gap remains present.

The service uploads unchanged bytes as ArrayBuffer under an owner/UUID path, supports stable retry upserts, validates base64, size, MIME/signature and account changes, and returns a canonical authenticated object URL. It normalizes existing signed references to tokenless canonical references. Credentials attach only to this project's authenticated endpoint for the specified owner, never external hosts/signed endpoints/another owner. Declared the already-installed base64-arraybuffer 1.0.2 as an exact direct dependency; lockfile and installed version match. Fifteen unit cases cover these boundaries. All 1,144 Jest tests / 97 suites, TypeScript, targeted lint and whitespace pass. All 38 migrations and the combined wear/Auth/avatar/session/media SQL chain pass on a fresh database. Fixed new SQL fixture ID collisions before adding the chained check to CI. Production security-advisor counts remain unchanged.

Executed the actual media-service source through installed Supabase JS against two disposable accounts and a 2,915,128-byte repository PNG (no user photos). Stable upload retry and authenticated/signed downloads preserve SHA-256 7c1728bfad35066ad916f46dd5c2f2411e24787021a891d5ed4671780ec297f3 exactly. Public/unauthenticated/other-owner reads, other-owner overwrite/list are denied. The existing delete-account Edge Function successfully removes the object in the new bucket. Of 13 planned live assertions, 12 executed: 11 passed and the immediate signed-link invalidation assertion FAILED; the final other-account assertion was not reached. All cleanup calls succeeded and independent SQL confirms zero readiness accounts/wardrobe objects, bucket public=false and limit10MiB.

The failure exposed a real access-semantic limit rather than missing object deletion. Supabase's [Smart CDN documentation](https://supabase.com/docs/guides/storage/cdn/smart-cdn) states that deletion invalidation can take up to 60 seconds, and token expiry does not purge cached responses; token lifetime and response caching are independent. The original failed assertion is preserved. The export helper now explicitly documents this limitation and is not used for routine rendering/persistence. No claim of instant signed-link revocation or completed image access integration is made, and no alternate public-photo path was added.

Evidence: `private-media-foundation.json`, `production-wardrobe-media.json`; verifier: `production-wardrobe-media.mjs`; policy fixture: `wardrobe-media.sql`. Next required work includes private native/Expo rendering, provider image access, durable offline source files, queue/save cutover, inline migration/recovery, thumbnails and realistic media capacity. Native artifacts are stale and original intermittent write reliability is unresolved. AI provider work remains deferred. Overall readiness remains uncertified.

### Private native/Expo image rendering integrated — 2026-10-17

Added owner-aware Image/ExpoImage components and connected 44 application image-import modules, including the daily-outfit prefetch hook found by the final broader inventory. A CI AST guard now checks named image imports across app source directories, allowing raw engines only inside the boundary component. Native/Expo props and refs continue to forward to their engines; public/static/inline sources retain their original source shape.

Private wardrobe sources require a current owner/session before reaching the native loader or cache. Signed inputs become canonical authenticated URLs, with current-owner Authorization headers and stable owner/path cache keys that contain no token. Mixed Expo prefetch separates public and private requests to avoid leaking authorization headers to external hosts. Native dimension reads use getSizeWithHeaders for owned private images; headerless native private prefetch returns false without making an unauthorized request.

Eleven new tests cover source metadata, signed normalization, absent/wrong-owner sessions, unchanged external/static/data sources, mixed arrays, mixed prefetch, token refresh, immediate logout/account switch and private dimension access. Expo's native mock normalizes an absent source to an empty array, so the rendering test checks both valid cleared forms. The full-repo lint run passed with zero errors/1,102 warnings before final small test/hook refinements; final targeted lint has zero errors/one existing daily-outfit ref-cleanup warning.
