### Native pending-photo staging and restart durability — 2026-10-17

Added pendingWardrobeMedia for the next save-pipeline cutover. It validates original bytes through the shared media reader, writes them to a temporary file in an owner/UUID Documents directory, moves to the final name and verifies size before returning. Same-content retries retain the file; reusing an ID for different content is rejected. File operations serialize, cleanup is explicitly owner-scoped, and account-revision checks prevent a late old-account stage from publishing into the next account. Failed moves/verification remove partial files. This service is native-only and is not yet invoked by addItem, queue acknowledgement or Auth cleanup.

Managed local references rebase to the current Documents container, so restored iOS paths need not retain an obsolete sandbox prefix. The image source boundary recognizes managed files, blocks missing/wrong-owner sessions, drops bearer headers for local reads and uses stable owner/photo cache identity. Existing unrelated local URIs are not silently treated as recoverable managed files. Refactored the existing upload service to share the same byte validation, retaining all 15 upload-service tests.

Ten staging tests cover byte preservation, cache-source copying, retry/content conflict, container relocation, move/size errors, account switches during reads/writes, cross-owner cleanup and unavailable storage/invalid paths. One renderer regression covers rebasing and ownership. A separate OS-filesystem verifier stages the 2,915,128-byte repository PNG, deletes its picker directory, renames the application container and launches a fresh Node process. The new process restores identical bytes, retries without replacement, blocks the wrong owner and removes the managed file. It executes actual source through an Expo-shaped adapter using real OS reads/writes/rename; it is explicitly not an Expo/native device runtime test. All generated fixture files are removed. Installed Android Expo implementation uses renameTo for file moves; iOS uses FileManager.moveItem after destination removal; the staging service avoids replacing an existing different photo.

All 1,166 Jest tests / 100 suites, TypeScript, targeted lint (zero warnings/errors), whitespace and image-import coverage pass. Both Android and iOS Hermes exports pass with this service integrated. The process-restart verifier is added to CI through test:media-durability; no remote CI run is claimed. Evidence: `pending-media-staging.json` records bundle/source hashes; `pending-media-os-durability.json` records process/filesystem assertions.

No production writes or provider calls occurred. Required next work remains queue-first metadata persistence before network activity, native staging/save/acknowledgement and Auth-cleanup integration, web durable storage, provider access, media migration/thumbnails/capacity and native runtime. The older server insertion/deletion-after-process-death race is not solved by staging alone. Existing production photo rows are unchanged; overall readiness remains uncertified.


### Queued wardrobe sync persistence barrier — 2026-10-17

Queued sync now explicitly writes the latest partialized wardrobe snapshot and flushes debounced storage before sending cloud mutations. Storage rejection or missing persistence retains pending actions and prevents cloud writes. An account revision check after persistence prevents the previous account from starting a request after switching users. Existing in-flight mutation and overlapping-sync tests now wait for actual cloud start.

Validation: all 1,170 Jest tests in 100 suites passed; TypeScript and whitespace checks passed. Targeted lint had zero errors and four existing warnings. Evidence: `readiness-evidence/wardrobe-queue-persistence-barrier.json`.

This protects the existing queued sync path. The direct garment-add path still needs queue-first staging integration; acknowledgement persistence, staged-file cleanup and native process-death testing remain open. No production requests or AI calls were made for this change.


### Initial garment creation uses queued sync — 2026-10-17

Removed the initial direct-insert path. Creation now atomically adds the optimistic garment and a stable-ID `add_item` action, then uses the shared serialized sync path and its local persistence barrier. Deletes and explicit favorite updates remain ordered behind creation. Preserved the previous direct path's `type` and `detection_confidence` fields in batch writes.

Validation: 1,170 tests across 100 suites passed; TypeScript and whitespace checks passed; targeted lint zero errors, six existing warnings. Added an integration regression proving initial creation cannot reach the cloud while the queued snapshot write remains unresolved. Updated old direct-insert tests to check the queue-first lifecycle. Native photo staging and cloud acknowledgement persistence/cleanup remain unfinished. This change does not prove restart-safe photo bytes, server-side deletion ordering after an unknown commit, or production capacity. No production writes or provider calls were made.


### Cloud acknowledgement persistence — 2026-10-17

Queued sync now waits for an explicit snapshot write and flush after removing acknowledged cloud actions. If this write fails, the original stable-ID actions are restored ahead of newer queued edits for a later retry. An account revision guard prevents failed old-account writes from restoring actions into the next account. This closes the acknowledgement barrier needed before staged photo cleanup can be connected; cleanup itself is not enabled.

Validation: all 1,174 tests in 100 suites passed, including delayed acknowledgement, disk rejection/retry, concurrent newer edits and account-switch regressions. TypeScript and whitespace checks passed; targeted lint zero errors and four existing warnings. Evidence: `readiness-evidence/wardrobe-acknowledgement-persistence.json`.

Persistence tests use mock storage; native process-death and actual photo lifecycle verification remain open. No production traffic or provider calls were made. The overall readiness goal remains unproven.


### Queued garment media upload integration — 2026-10-17

Queued local-wins garment writes now prepare original and thumbnail images before batch upsert. Device/data sources upload through the existing validated ArrayBuffer helper; managed pending paths rebase to the current Documents container. Separate original/thumbnail object names prevent one overwriting the other, while identical sources reuse the original. Existing remote catalog URLs pass through; own signed wardrobe references normalize to authenticated references and foreign owner references reject.

Confirmed record writes return prepared image references to sync. The local merge preserves intervening favorite/name edits and newly selected photos while updating unchanged image fields and matching outfit snapshot/preview references. Failed media preparation or record writes retain queued work and original references. Wear logs referencing an unconfirmed garment creation wait for a later attempt, preserving the server counter dependency.

Validation: all 1,184 Jest tests in 102 suites passed, TypeScript and whitespace checks passed; targeted lint zero errors/four existing warnings. The image import boundary check and actual-source OS staging durability test also passed (2,915,128 bytes after picker eviction and container relocation in a new Node process, using an Expo-shaped filesystem adapter). Evidence: `readiness-evidence/garment-media-sync.json`.

The new cloud integration itself has mock-backed regression evidence, not a live or device-runtime pass. Native staging is still not invoked by addItem; initial picker/cache sources remain vulnerable before upload. Staged-file cleanup remains disconnected, and cloud saved_outfits embedded snapshots still need normalization. No production, provider, or account operations were performed in this change.


### Initial native garment save stages photo bytes — 2026-10-17

Native local/data photos now stage through `stageNativeWardrobeImage` before addItem publishes either the optimistic item or its pending action. Staged references use the authenticated owner. Distinct thumbnails receive separate file IDs; identical original/thumbnail sources share one staged file. Auth revision and the current wardrobe cap are checked again after file IO, preventing late old-account publication and concurrent saves exceeding the limit. File failure creates no new item or upload action. Existing remote catalog-only saves retain the synchronous queue-publication path.

Validation: 1,191 tests in 103 suites passed; TypeScript, whitespace, image import boundary and actual-source OS staging durability checks passed. Targeted lint zero errors/one existing warning. New regressions include deferred file work, disk failure, account switch, separate/reused thumbnails, concurrent cap and missing owner. An additional test uses the actual addItem and staging helper together with an Expo-shaped in-memory filesystem adapter. Evidence: `readiness-evidence/native-garment-save-staging.json`.

The separate durability harness proves unchanged 2,915,128-byte data after picker eviction/container relocation across a new Node process with an OS filesystem adapter; it does not prove native process-death behavior. Simulator/device restart verification, browser binary storage, cloud saved-outfit snapshot normalization and reference-aware cleanup remain open. Cleanup is not enabled; failed or rejected saves can leave managed owner-scoped files until safe garbage collection is implemented. No production traffic, provider calls or account changes were made.


### Saved outfit snapshot media normalization — 2026-10-17

Cloud outfit saves now prepare both `image` and `imageUrl` fields before record writes, preserving each input field convention. Input fields are copied before session lookup so caller edits during asynchronous work cannot change the saved look. Media failure or auth revision change prevents the record write. ### Native iOS private-image restart verification and auth transport deadline — 2026-10-17

Built the current arm64 Release app and installed it only on the isolated `AIWardrobe avatar audit` iOS 26.5 simulator. The user's original signed-in simulator was untouched. One disposable account and one private 2,915,128-byte repository image/garment were set up server-side; credentials were entered through the actual sign-in UI, with no session or app-storage injection.

The first native sign-in failed with Expo fetch / NSURLErrorDomain -1005, network connection lost. Sanitized OS evidence includes a QUIC connection timing out after 81.892 seconds; Supabase had no active session afterward. This failure is preserved and its exact network root cause remains unresolved.

Added a 15-second deadline only for this project's auth HTTP transport, including response body completion, preserving incoming cancellation and clearing listeners/timers. Late responses cannot reach the SDK after timeout; the wrapper does not retry auth mutations or change other HTTP origins/data/storage transport. The installed Supabase client regression confirms timeout returns an error with no session and no sign-in retry. This bounds auth HTTP calls, not the entire auth initialization/lock/enrichment pipeline.

The deadline-enabled Release build signed in successfully through the normal UI and completed onboarding. The private fixture photo initially appeared blank, then visibly loaded in Closet. After process termination and relaunch, the app restored the session and visibly displayed the same photo. UI sign-out returned to the unauthenticated language gate; a further cold restart after fixture cleanup displayed Sign In. The successful second attempt verifies compatibility of the new wrapper with native successful auth; it does not prove the original network issue fixed. No accurate image-load latency was measured, and the delay is not considered a performance pass.

Local verification: 1,210 Jest tests in 106 suites passed, including nine auth-transport tests; TypeScript, targeted lint (zero warnings/errors) and whitespace checks passed. Both current iOS native builds succeeded; final artifact hashes and observation limits are in `readiness-evidence/native-private-media-restart.json`.

Storage fixture and temporary Auth account were removed, local fixture manifest deleted, and independent SQL confirmed 0 disposable accounts, 109 garments, 13 saved outfits and 0 wardrobe media objects. No user photo was used and no explicit AI-generation or purchase action was invoked. Home background requests were not audited as provider-capacity evidence.

An initially suspected Profile count mismatch was reclassified after source inspection: its Saved Looks gallery intentionally aggregates garments and liked shop products as well as outfits; that established behavior was preserved.

This test used a server-seeded garment and proves native authenticated rendering/session restoration only. It does not exercise a new picker photo through staging/upload/restart: active photo-add routes invoke the deferred AI classifier, while the manual QuickAddItem component currently has no call site. ### Automatic native thumbnails with measured codec output — 2026-10-17

Added exact-pinned Expo ImageManipulator 57.0.21, compatible with the installed Expo 57 range, and updated iOS autolinking/Podfile.lock. Expo remains 57.0.20 and React Native 0.86.3. Local native garment saves now generate a separate staged PNG preview when a thumbnail is missing or aliases the original; distinct supplied thumbnails remain supported. Both original and thumbnail references enter the durable upload action only after staging. Original bytes are retained unchanged. Encoding failure does not publish an original-only garment.

The generator limits the longest edge to 512 pixels without upscaling, checks dimensions/aspect ratio, and rejects inputs above 50 million pixels before manipulation. It scopes sources to durable owner-managed originals, validates cache-output paths before cleanup, and releases native image/context resources. A serialized queue prevents simultaneous decoder workloads and captures auth revision before queueing; same-account logout/relogin cannot revive stale queued jobs. Cache deletion follows successful staging or failed encoding/staging and never deletes a durable source.

Native codec verification used a standalone explicit ENTRY_FILE harness on the isolated iOS 26.5 audit simulator, with actual staging/generator source and installed native codec. The 2,915,128-byte repository photo produced a 472,270-byte 384×512 PNG (83.8% smaller) in one 664 ms sample. The 41,271-byte icon produced a 23,256-byte 512×512 PNG in one 31 ms sample. Original bytes were unchanged. Read-only Pillow inspection of native output confirmed nonopaque icon alpha values remained; the output was not flattened to fully opaque pixels. The harness visibly displayed both outputs. Evidence: `native-thumbnail-codec.json`; retained inspection copies are under `/tmp/aiwardrobe-native-thumbnail-artifacts/`.

After codec verification, connected the generator to native garment creation and added serialization. Thirteen service tests cover size/aspect bounds, ownership, auth changes, failure cleanup, resource release, overlapping jobs and queue recovery; save-path regressions verify thumbnail failure creates no upload action. All 1,231 tests in 109 suites, TypeScript, whitespace, media import boundary and original OS staging durability checks passed. Lint has zero errors and one existing items-slice warning. The serial wrapper is regression-tested separately; the native codec sample timing is not a benchmark of concurrent saves.

The final normal arm64 iOS Release build succeeded and its bundle contains the thumbnail integration while excluding the audit heading. Cleared only the isolated audit app's harness data, reinstalled the normal build and observed its language gate. The user's original simulator was untouched. Android Hermes export passed; no Android native runtime pass is claimed. Evidence and artifact hashes: `automatic-native-thumbnails.json`.

Dependency audit is NOT clean. A fresh feed reports 115 findings (3 critical, 87 high, 24 moderate, 1 low). A control using the current lock graph with the new module removed reports the same severity counts; finding objects differ, so this is not an assertion of identical graphs or a historical pre-install baseline. The new module has no direct audit finding. Critical module names include @appium/support, proxy-addr and a nested shell-quote; reachability and remediation remain open. Preserved full reports: `npm-audit-thumbnail-added.json` and `npm-audit-thumbnail-control.json`. Earlier smaller audit totals are historical and must not be used as the current state.

Still required: JPEG/HEIC/orientation and Android runtime verification, heavy-image memory stress, actual picker-to-cloud/restart behavior, existing image backfill, browser media storage, safe staged-original cleanup and measured media HTTP capacity. This native harness does not prove an end-to-end authenticated UI upload or 10,000 DAU readiness.

Installing the official driver source at release commit `1be253af959bbd29427166a910a78a38fdd66185` failed its preparation/typecheck because Appium driver/support declarations were unavailable in that build context. This is an unsuccessful remediation attempt, not a security pass.

No production deployment or UI automation was performed for this dependency experiment. The critical dependency findings remain open. Added a standalone advisory regression diagnostic at `scripts/validation/dependency-security.test.mjs`; it is intentionally not a passing CI gate while affected installed copies remain. Its ZIP fixtures are confined to disposable temporary directories.

Post-restore verification: npm ci completed successfully; package files still match the snapshot exactly. TypeScript and all 1,231 app tests in 109 suites passed again, as did the media import guard and whitespace check. The standalone dependency diagnostic has six passing and three failing cases: both installed proxy-addr copies accept the spoofed forwarded address under the malformed mapped-subnet configuration, and the nested shell-quote copy accepts the newline operator. The root shell-quote rejects it. Both ZIP copies reject this particular symlink-followed-by-file fixture without writing the outside file; this limited fixture does not reproduce or clear the complete ZIP advisory. Evidence: `readiness-evidence/dependency-advisory-regressions.tap`. No injected shell command is executed by these diagnostics.


### Thumbnail cache cleanup path boundary — 2026-10-17

Invalid results never reach staging or cleanup; native resource release still runs. Seven new cases cover cache root, directory suffix, normalized traversal and encoded escape shapes. Normal thumbnail staging and cleanup remain covered by the existing cases. This validates the URI boundary, not arbitrary native filesystem symlink/directory substitution.

### Production private-image delivery diagnostic FAILED — 2026-10-17

Ran a bounded diagnostic against the real project with two disposable authenticated owners. Actual app media upload source and the installed Supabase SDK created each owner's 2,915,128-byte original and native-generated 472,270-byte preview. Anonymous and foreign-owner authenticated reads were rejected. Six sequential original reads returned exact fixture bytes; p95 full-body completion was 8,356.01 ms from this test machine.

The preview ramp scheduled 2, then 5, then 10 requests/second for ten seconds each, with a 15-second full-response timeout and at most 20 active requests. At 2 RPS, 20/20 returned exact bytes, zero failures/drops, p95 3,200.51 ms and p99 3,382.85 ms. At 5 RPS, 32 of 50 scheduled requests were issued: 26 returned exact bytes, six timed out, and 18 were dropped by the client concurrency cap. p95 was 15,001.52 ms and p99 15,002.54 ms including timeouts. The ramp stopped after this failed stage; 10 RPS was not run. HTTP/body/hash measurements include the local machine and its network. Client drops are not server HTTP errors, and this run cannot attribute the bottleneck to Supabase, its CDN or the client network. This contradicts a claim of measured acceptable image delivery and does not establish 10,000 DAU readiness.

Preserved the failed run in `readiness-evidence/production-media-delivery.json`. All four objects and both disposable accounts were removed through Storage/Auth APIs. An independent production SQL query confirmed zero remaining fixture accounts and objects for both recorded IDs. No customer rows were written. The harness now names future reports by timestamp to preserve this failure, and records separate header/body timing for a future diagnostic; those timing fields were not captured in this original run. Syntax and whitespace passed. No passing rerun is claimed. Further testing needs to distinguish client/network constraints from origin delivery and use a sustained representative media workload.


### Instrumented media repeat and correlated delivery delay — 2026-10-17

Repeated the bounded live image diagnostic with separate header/body timing and response request IDs. The original failed report remains preserved. The repeat again failed its full offered-load target: 2 RPS completed 20/20 with p95 3,044.49 ms; 5 RPS completed 50/50 with p95 9,662.03 ms; 10 RPS issued/completed 35 of 100 scheduled requests and dropped 65 at the 20-active-request client cap, p95 10,984.28 ms. No issued requests timed out in this repeat and all returned exact bytes, but this is not a passing offered-load result. Original reads completed 6/6 with p95 5,578.90 ms.

At 10 RPS, p95 header receipt was 4,296.77 ms and p95 body completion after headers was 10,351.64 ms (percentiles are independent and must not be summed). The slowest matched preview request, `01a14882-2051-7176-b61d-0a2258b885a4`, received HTTP 200 headers in 332.09 ms and took another 10,899.28 ms to receive/hash its 472,270 bytes, total 11,231.37 ms. Its exact edge-log request ID matched a cache HIT with recorded `response.origin_time=12`. This is direct evidence that this request's multi-second delay occurred after response headers, rather than waiting for a multi-second database operation. It does not distinguish CDN-to-client transport from local network/runtime constraints or explain every earlier timeout. Request/header/body correlation is partial: one of the five slowest IDs was present in the available edge-log sample. The prior run's Storage sample likewise does not cover every request.

Supabase log field references were checked at https://supabase.com/docs/guides/observability/log-field-reference and Storage log querying at https://supabase.com/docs/guides/storage/debugging/logs. Node 22.23.2 reported no configured HTTP/HTTPS proxy and no enabled environment proxy flag; this does not rule out other network intermediaries. No app/provider configuration was changed on this evidence. Next capacity work requires a controlled second network/load location and more representative owners/object cardinality; repeatedly reading four objects from one machine is not a 10,000 DAU load proof.

Evidence: `production-media-delivery-2026-10-17T06-16-23-109Z.json`, `media-delivery-instrumented-edge-sample.json`, `media-delivery-origin-log-sample.json`. Both temporary accounts and all four files were removed; independent SQL confirmed zero remaining fixture accounts and objects. No customer records were modified. Harness syntax/whitespace passed. This run remains marked failed.


### Native photo size preflight before buffering — 2026-10-17

Native staging previously checked its 10 MiB limit only after fetch.arrayBuffer(), allowing an oversized file to allocate its full body before rejection. For file: sources, staging now reads filesystem metadata first and rejects missing files/directories and noninteger, zero, negative or excessive sizes before native fetch or base64 copying. The auth revision is rechecked after metadata lookup. Existing post-read byte/encoding validation remains in place. This is a native file preflight, not a streaming bound for content:/blob: sources or a guarantee against a file changing after the metadata check.

Eight new regressions verify invalid/excessive sizes, missing/directory input and account changes during metadata lookup without any fetch/write. All 19 staging tests and all 1,253 app tests in 111 suites passed; TypeScript, targeted lint and whitespace passed. The real-OS durability harness created a 67,108,864-byte sparse file and verified actual staging source rejected it without calling the body reader. Normal 2,915,128-byte original staging, picker eviction, container relocation, retry in a fresh Node process, owner isolation and cleanup still pass. Evidence: `pending-media-os-durability.json`. The OS check uses an Expo-shaped filesystem adapter, not a new device runtime run. No production data or provider configuration was changed. Media delivery capacity and the other readiness gaps remain open.

The editor now uses a synchronous ref guard around every save path, with a disabled/busy accessible button until completion. Custom save callbacks explicitly support Promise<void>, are awaited, and their errors receive the normal error feedback with retry enabled. Success haptics follow confirmed create/custom persistence. New-item late completions and errors are suppressed after an auth revision change. Existing edit persistence still updates local state only after server confirmation.

Three new rendered regressions cover direct same-tick onPress calls sharing one new-item save, rejected async callback/retry without false success, and old-account completion after logout. Existing failed/successful edit cases still pass. All five editor tests and all 1,256 app tests in 111 suites passed; TypeScript and whitespace passed. Targeted lint has zero errors and eleven existing warnings in the editor/test files. No native device run, production write, AI request or statistics-wide certification was performed. This prevents the tested overlapping save taps, not all duplicate creation across restarts, multiple devices or externally invoked addItem calls. Media capacity and other readiness gaps remain open.

### Saved shop cache ordering across account resets — 2026-10-17

The saved-shop store previously ran independent best-effort writes against the shared savedInspo key. A late old-account write could repopulate that key after logout, and a hydration read could undo a newer save. Its reset now invalidates pending reads and queued old writes, marks disk data untrusted and queues removal behind writes already in flight. New-account writes share that queue and run after cleanup. The barrier is established before notifying store subscribers, including synchronous subscribers that immediately hydrate or save. A removal failure leaves disk untrusted so this process cannot reload the old data. A successful current-generation write restores trust. Failed saves likewise do not permit hydration to replace current in-memory likes with an older disk snapshot.

The general persisted-data cleanup now awaits this store's ordered queue after its memory reset, so a late likes write cannot survive behind the registry's multiRemove. Existing legacy key format and best-effort save contract are retained. Updated auth/persistence comments to make the saved-shop disk-cleanup side effect explicit rather than describing all resets as memory-only.

Seven focused store cases cover delayed old write/new write ordering, failed removal, late hydration, failed save, reset during old-action notification, fresh writes during reset notification and hydration during reset notification. An additional integration regression exercises the actual clearAllPersistedUserData helper with a write completed after registry removal. All 1,271 tests in 114 suites passed, as did TypeScript and whitespace. Targeted lint has zero errors and eighteen lazy-require warnings across the persistence utility/test; the new store/test code is clean. These tests use the Jest AsyncStorage boundary, not native filesystem/process-death testing. Evidence: saved-shop-cache-ordering.json.

This closes the tested in-process cache races. The key still lacks durable owner metadata, and the trust flag is process-local: interrupted cleanup across process death, legacy-cache ownership, broader SDK account-switch disk isolation, best-effort save failure durability and old global cleanup overlapping a new login remain open. No new native build or production request was made for this fix; the latest APK/iOS binaries predate it. This is not a full shared-device or 10,000 DAU certification.


### Paired production Storage transport diagnostic — 2026-10-17

Each successful response required the exact byte length and SHA-256. Each stage scheduled reads for ten seconds, with a twenty-request concurrency cap. This is a short repeated-object diagnostic, not a sustained workload or 10,000 DAU certification.

| Location | Target reads/sec | Successful / planned | Failed reads | Client cap drops | Total response p95 |
| --- | ---: | ---: | ---: | ---: | ---: |
| Edge eu-central-1 | 2 | 20 / 20 | 0 | 0 | 2,334 ms |
| Edge eu-central-1 | 5 | 50 / 50 | 0 | 0 | 74 ms |
| Edge eu-central-1 | 10 | 100 / 100 | 0 | 0 | 50 ms |
| Mac | 2 | 20 / 20 | 0 | 0 | 4,288 ms |
| Mac | 5 | 22 / 50 | 10 | 18 | 15,002 ms |

The private session manifest and temporary image were also removed. No AI call, payment or customer account change was involved.

### Durable saved-shop cache ownership — 2026-10-17

Saved-shop records now store a version, authenticated owner ID and item list together under savedInspo. Hydration requires a matching owner and supported version. Unknown startup identity and logged-out actions cannot load or save likes. Auth login/logout events synchronously invalidate old reads/writes and clear visible likes when identity changes; same-account refresh preserves memory. A late in-flight write still carries its captured old owner tag, so a new account cannot read it even if it survives interrupted cleanup. Existing ordered persistence and cleanup barriers remain.

Unowned legacy arrays are not assigned to the next login: the original format supplies no reliable ownership proof. Such likes will not appear in the upgraded app. They remain on disk until normal cleanup or a new save replaces them; no attempt was made to infer ownership from the current account. This is a visible compatibility cost required to avoid disclosing an unknown account's saved data.

Eight additional tests cover tagged writes, same-account refresh, direct SDK identity changes, foreign records after interrupted cleanup, legacy arrays, same-owner disk hydration, logout/anonymous actions, an old write finishing after account switch and a fresh isolated module registry before/after identity establishment. All fifteen store tests and four cleanup integration tests passed as part of all 1,279 tests in 114 suites. TypeScript and whitespace passed; targeted lint has zero errors and six lazy-require warnings in test files. The initial full run had one test-import failure (CommonJS mock default shape), corrected before the final passing full suite. Evidence: [saved-shop-owner-validation.json](readiness-evidence/saved-shop-owner-validation.json).

This is actual application-store logic exercised at the mocked AsyncStorage boundary, including a fresh module registry, not a native process-death test. No production requests or native build were made; existing binaries predate this change. Broader cache ownership/global cleanup coordination, best-effort save durability and mutations racing initial hydration remain open. These results do not establish complete account isolation or 10,000 DAU readiness.


### Preserve saved likes during initial hydration — 2026-10-17

Confirmed that the initial toggle/remove could persist an empty in-memory list before the account's existing disk cache loaded, losing earlier saved likes. Toggles and removals now await one shared initial hydration for the current identity, then derive their change from the loaded state. Concurrent first operations apply against the latest memory state. Identity changes invalidate waiting operations, and explicit replacements invalidate earlier reads. Once loaded, focus hydration is idempotent and does not reload stale disk data over newer memory.

A failed initial read or malformed JSON leaves hydration incomplete. Toggle/remove reject without replacing the unread cache, allowing a later retry. Explicit setItems/clearAll retain their intentional replacement behavior. Existing best-effort write semantics remain; this change does not claim durable saving after an AsyncStorage write failure.

Inspo now derives saved/removed feedback from the completed toggle result rather than a pre-load snapshot, suppresses old-account feedback and catches load failures with a localized retry message. Profile waits for removal before closing the preview and presents the same localized error if initial loading fails. English, Russian and Uzbek messages were added and their JSON/key presence checked. The changed screen callbacks were typechecked, linted and source-reviewed, not rendered/native tested this turn.

Seven added store tests plus the updated initial-hydration race cover concurrent saves, toggling/removing existing persisted likes, read failure and retry, malformed JSON retention, identity change during read and explicit replacement during read. All 22 saved-shop tests and four cleanup integration tests passed within all 1,286 tests in 114 suites. TypeScript and whitespace passed; targeted lint has zero errors and 21 warnings. Evidence: [saved-shop-initial-hydration.json](readiness-evidence/saved-shop-initial-hydration.json).

No production request or native build was made. Existing binaries predate these changes. Native restart/process-death validation, best-effort write durability, broader account cleanup races, production media performance, store integration, dependency findings and deferred AI-provider configuration remain open. Full correctness and 10,000 DAU readiness are not established.


### Confirm saved-shop mutations before success — 2026-10-17

Saved-shop persistence previously caught write errors and fulfilled the action promise after publishing optimistic memory. Inspo could therefore report Saved, or Profile close a removed item's preview, despite disk retaining the previous list. Saves, removals, explicit replacements and clear now return their actual write failure to the caller and publish visible state only after AsyncStorage confirms completion for the same identity. Each queued mutation derives from the latest confirmed list inside the ordered persistence operation. Failed work leaves memory unchanged and does not poison subsequent work or cleanup. Account/reset guards remain before and after storage I/O.

The existing Inspo/Profile callers already await and catch these promises; verified there are no additional application callers bypassing that handling. Their localized retry wording now describes an update failure, covering initial-load and write failures. No success result is manufactured when setItem rejects. This supersedes the best-effort saved-shop behavior described in earlier historical sections; other best-effort stores and global cleanup behavior are not changed.

Five new tests and the updated write-failure regression verify delayed confirmation, failed removal, queued success after an earlier failure, overlapping toggles of the same item and failed replacement/clear. All 27 store cases and four cleanup integration cases passed within all 1,291 tests in 114 suites. TypeScript, translation JSON/key checks and whitespace passed. Targeted lint has zero errors and three lazy-require warnings in the fresh-module test. Evidence: [saved-shop-confirmed-writes.json](readiness-evidence/saved-shop-confirmed-writes.json).

Verification uses actual store code against mocked AsyncStorage, not a native disk-full/process-death test. AsyncStorage fulfillment is the application boundary checked here; no filesystem fsync or resolution of an ambiguous platform write failure is claimed. No production request or new native binary was made. Native lifecycle, broader cache/global cleanup coordination, production media performance, dependency findings, store integration and deferred AI-provider work remain open. The overall goal remains unverified.

### Owner-scoped calendar storage — 2026-10-17

Calendar/trip plans previously shared the outfitLogs key even though async operations checked auth revision. A new account or cold process could therefore read another owner's surviving calendar after interrupted cleanup. The actual calendar storage service now reads/writes outfitLogs::<encoded authenticated owner>, captures owner plus revision and checks both around storage I/O. Unknown/logged-out identities read an empty result without touching disk and cannot write. A mutation callback that changes identity is checked again before writing. Late old-account writes remain restricted to the captured old-owner key and reject their completion after an identity change.

The legacy unowned outfitLogs record cannot be reliably attributed to a current account. Normal calendar reads do not reveal it and new saves do not overwrite it: it remains intact under the legacy key until the existing explicit logout/account-delete cleanup removes it. Legacy plans will not appear automatically in the new owner-scoped calendar. Recovery requires evidence of ownership; no ownership guess or silent import was made. The persistence registry now clears all new owner keys by their shared exported prefix, with cleanup integration covering both A and B keys alongside the legacy key.

Seven added service cases verify independent calendars across direct account switches, legacy preservation, anonymous denial, late old-owner write isolation, stale read rejection, identity change inside a mutation and collision-safe owner encoding. All twelve service tests, calendar hook tests and cleanup integration passed within all 1,298 tests in 114 suites. The first focused run exposed two hook fixtures that had never established an auth identity; they now explicitly log in rather than weakening the production guard. TypeScript and whitespace passed; targeted lint has zero errors and eighteen lazy-require warnings. Evidence: [calendar-owner-storage.json](readiness-evidence/calendar-owner-storage.json).

These are actual service/helper tests at mocked AsyncStorage, not native calendar restart or UI verification. No production request or new native build was made; the latest Android package and earlier iOS package predate this calendar change. Global cleanup overlapping a new login, other unowned caches, native calendar lifecycle, legacy recovery and all outstanding media/store/dependency/provider gates remain open. Full correctness and 10,000 DAU readiness are not established.


### Guard stale logout and cleanup completion — 2026-10-17

Confirmed that logout could await SDK/IAP/cleanup work and then unconditionally replace a newer session with logged-out state. Logout now captures its emitted auth revision and checks it after sign-out, after IAP logout and before final state publication. It passes that revision to the persistence helper, so a cleanup prepared for an old identity cannot start against a newer one.

The cleanup helper checks its captured revision at entry, after draining calendar writes, after upload/wardrobe cleanup, after key scanning, before global memory reset and before secure-key deletion. If identity changed, it stops issuing subsequent cleanup stages. This prevents a stale key scan from triggering the later bulk deletion, and prevents delayed deletion completion from clearing newer in-memory state or starting secure-key removal. It does not cancel already-issued work.

Five regressions cover stale cleanup at entry, identity change during key scan, a delayed registered bulk removal, newer login during SDK sign-out and newer login during local cleanup. All 1,303 tests in 114 suites passed; after refining the delayed-removal fixture to intercept the global bulk call specifically, all 43 focused auth/cleanup tests passed again. TypeScript and whitespace passed; targeted lint has zero errors and 49 warnings. The initial assertion counted unrelated per-store removals, which was corrected without weakening the global-deletion assertion. Evidence: [stale-cleanup-revision-guards.json](readiness-evidence/stale-cleanup-revision-guards.json).

Reviewed current official Supabase changelog and Auth documentation through the connected documentation search. No SDK API signature, sign-out scope, database policy or hosted function was changed. No production auth call, customer account change or native build was made. The changes are application guards tested with SDK/AsyncStorage mocks.

This narrows stale-continuation bugs; it is not complete cleanup/login serialization. An already-issued storage removal can still delete newly written disk data, and an already-issued SDK sign-out or IAP logout can still affect the underlying session/provider. The revised test intentionally verifies only preservation of new memory and avoidance of later secure deletion after the delayed removal. Account-deletion concurrency, native overlap tests, other caches and all existing capacity/media/store/dependency/provider gaps remain open. The goal remains unverified.


### Same-Mac image transport comparison — 2026-10-17

Ran a bounded live diagnostic against one new marked disposable owner and one private PNG: Node fetch, system curl explicitly requesting HTTP/1.1, and curl requesting HTTP/2. Each client scheduled 50 reads at 5/sec over ten seconds, with twenty active reads maximum and a fifteen-second request timeout. Every successful read required exact bytes and SHA-256. The 788,309-byte fixture matches the earlier paired-run hash (39b14e9041fca06639a34a1c04420d57cc57776bec23c524f21cd88f1a4a3bd7). Credentials stayed in memory; curl received the bearer header over stdin rather than process arguments or a header file. A public recovery ledger recorded fixture IDs before creation.

| Client | Successful / planned | Failed issued reads | Client-cap drops | Response p95 |
| --- | ---: | ---: | ---: | ---: |
| Node fetch v22.23.2 | 0 / 50 | 20 | 30 | 15,004 ms |
| curl HTTP/1.1 | 27 / 50 | 0 | 23 | 10,823 ms |
| curl HTTP/2 | 35 / 50 | 0 | 15 | 9,687 ms |

All twenty Node reads received HTTP 200 headers but then hit confirmed TimeoutError before body completion. This run retains failure categories; it does not retroactively classify earlier runs' unrecorded failures. All 62 completed curl reads matched the expected hash and size. Curl reported actual protocols 1.1 and 2 respectively, with successful-body p95 of 9,706 ms and 7,058 ms. Neither curl workload met its scheduled target because the concurrency cap dropped requests. All three stages therefore fail capacity acceptance; HTTP success for issued requests cannot hide dropped work. Node is worse in this run, but slow delivery and drops also occur outside Node.

These clients ran sequentially in fixed order against a repeated object. Cache/time/order effects are uncontrolled. Each curl read launched a fresh process/connection while Node used its normal fetch dispatcher/pool. Wall timing includes curl launch overhead; Node wire protocol, remote IP and CDN cache status were not captured. This does not isolate one library defect, prove an ISP/CDN cause or establish the native mobile stack's behavior. The earlier same-project remote runtime result remains a useful location contrast, not proof of current end-user capacity. No app transport replacement or workaround was introduced.

Storage API removal/listing and Auth deletion checks passed. Independent SQL then confirmed zero fixture users, sessions, identities, profiles and Storage objects. Temporary response/image directories were removed. No Edge Function was deployed and no AI call, payment or customer-data change was involved; authorized production writes consisted of the marked temporary account and image. Evidence: [transport measurements](readiness-evidence/media-transport-bf223d46-71f0-4027-a88b-5cb7489fee01.json). Executed source and its hash are retained with the report. Syntax, dry-plan and whitespace checks passed. No app logic changed; the last full suite remains 1,303 tests/114 suites, not rerun for this diagnostic.

Production media delivery remains a readiness blocker. A controlled order/connection comparison and actual native-client measurements are still needed, alongside sustained/many-owner workload verification and the other open correctness, subscription, dependency and deferred-provider gates. The 10,000 DAU objective is not achieved.


### Android native React Native image transport workload — 2026-10-17

Built and ran an explicit-entry Android API 34 arm64 harness using actual React Native fetch and installed Expo Crypto, with the same 788,309-byte private PNG hash as the earlier transport comparisons. The disposable emulator uses the Mac's network route. One marked disposable Supabase owner/object supplied the fixture. The bearer token was delivered through stdin into a private app file, not source, APK, command arguments or logs. The native workload FAILED: 50 planned reads at 5/sec, twenty active maximum, fifteen-second operation deadline; twenty issued reads timed out, zero completed with verified integrity, and thirty scheduled reads were dropped at the concurrency cap. All twenty received HTTP 200. Total elapsed was 18,828 ms and p95 was 15,024 ms. This demonstrates failure outside desktop Node fetch, but does not locate the underlying defect or global service capacity limit. The deadline covers response acquisition, arrayBuffer reading and SHA-256 verification. The harness did not retain the failing substage, so these are operation timeouts, not proof that every timeout occurred in network-body transfer. React Native fetch-resolution timing (p95 3,391 ms) is explicitly named separately from network headers/TTFB; implementation semantics can differ. No Expo Image rendering, visual UI, real native-thumbnail workload, physical mobile network, iOS comparison, native wire protocol or CDN-cache claim follows. The PNG is the matched uncompressed transport control, not a measured native preview. Root cause and representative-app performance remain unproven.

### Native diagnostic preflight correction and review limit — 2026-10-17

Installed source inspection clarified that Expo 57.0.20 replaces global fetch with its streaming implementation by default (unless EXPO_PUBLIC_USE_RN_FETCH opts out). The instrumented native report confirmed defaultFetchIsExpo=true. Earlier wording describing a React Native global fetch workload therefore refers to Expo's default fetch running on React Native, not proof that the XMLHttpRequest-backed implementation was used. No application transport setting or provider was changed.

Prepared an Expo/XHR/Expo comparison with per-request failure phases and native Crypto preflight. The installed diagnostic failed before any of these network stages executed. Preserved that report separately as native-media-preflight-failure-004b5660-37ea-4b52-ab41-febeb0d02fb1.json. The actual exception detail was not retained beyond a generic failure, so its exclusive cause is not proved by that report. Inspection found a concrete compatibility problem: expo-crypto 57.0.2's public API accepts BufferSource, but its installed Android digest binding takes TypedArray and the fallback JS wrapper forwards the supplied input. The audit had supplied a bare ArrayBuffer.

Three regressions verify a known SHA-256, the 788,309-byte preflight against an independent reference, and error propagation using an Android-shaped native mock. No test credential remains in the source or APK.

### Bind account deletion to its initiating identity — 2026-10-17

The client deletion flow previously obtained a session, then invoked delete-account without an explicit JWT and unconditionally continued local logout/cleanup after the response. The installed Supabase auth-fetch wrapper performs asynchronous token lookup. A newer login between those steps could supply a different token, and a late old deletion response could clear newer local state.

Deletion now captures the selected owner and auth revision, requires an authenticated matching SDK session, and passes that captured session JWT explicitly in the invoke Authorization header. Checks before invocation and after response prevent stale success/failure handling. After the operation emits its own logout, it adopts that new revision and checks it around SDK/IAP/persistence awaits and before final state publication. The persistence helper receives the captured revision. A current missing/mismatched session rejects without invoking deletion; an old operation never publishes its failure into a newer account.

Profile's delete callback no longer independently removes userToken after awaiting deletion, and suppresses old-account error alerts. Profile logout no longer waits for an independent token removal before starting logout, and now awaits the guarded logout action before ending its performance span. The persistence registry already owns token cleanup. Normal logout completion also explicitly clears its loading flag. Profile callback changes were source-reviewed/typechecked/linted; no rendered Profile UI claim is made.

Eight new auth cases cover token binding, mismatched SDK ownership, identity change during session lookup, late success/failure and new login during SDK signOut, IAP logout or local cleanup. Two additional tests run the actual installed Supabase client/functions/auth-fetch chain with a mocked network boundary: an invoke-level captured JWT survives a newer asynchronous SDK token result; omitting the header reproduces selection of that newer token. These are no-network tests, not a production deletion exercise. Inspected installed FunctionsClient invoke-header priority and fetchWithAuth's preservation of an existing Authorization header, alongside current official function/auth docs.

All 1,316 tests in 116 suites passed, plus TypeScript and whitespace. Targeted lint has zero errors and 43 warnings. Evidence: [delete-account-identity-isolation.json](readiness-evidence/delete-account-identity-isolation.json). No hosted function, schema, production account or native binary was changed. Existing binaries predate these source fixes.

This closes the tested request-identity and stale-continuation defects, not all account transition races. Already-issued SDK signOut/IAP/storage operations can still affect shared underlying state after a new login; full entry/cleanup serialization and native lifecycle verification remain open. The corrected native transport rerun still requires approval-review availability. Production media performance, remaining store/subscription/dependency/provider work and full 10,000 DAU acceptance remain unverified.

### Password-recovery account isolation — 2026-10-17

Inspection of remaining account-transition races found that both password-recovery deep-link formats assigned a new session directly without updating the cache identity or clearing the previous profile. Two regression tests reproduced subscribers observing the new owner session with the old profile and old authEvents owner. This can happen without a registered SDK observer, including initialization transitions.

Both formats now share a recovery-specific session publication helper. It emits the new cache identity and resets previous-account memory before publishing the session, removes a mismatched profile, and preserves a known profile/caches for same-account recovery. Recovery still avoids login analytics, IAP identification, profile fetch and trial setup. The tests inspect every new-session publication, rather than only the final store snapshot.

Verification: 47 auth tests passed after the two reproduced failures; full suite 1,319 tests / 116 suites passed; TypeScript passed. The normal Android release build passed, packaged auth source-map contents match the current file, and the media audit marker is absent. This is source/store behavior plus packaging evidence; SDK exchange and cache-reset dependencies are mocked in these regressions, and no production email-recovery or native recovery UI session was executed. The earlier normal APK evidence is historical and predates this fix.

[Evidence](readiness-evidence/recovery-account-isolation.json). The already-issued SDK/IAP/storage cleanup overlap remains unresolved, along with the broader capacity/media/store/provider/security gates. The goal remains active.


### Account-session publication ordering — 2026-10-17

The previous recovery fix exposed a related gap in normal authentication: login/registration published the new session before authEvents changed the cache owner; startup restoration also skipped resetting previous-user caches. Three subscriber-boundary regressions reproduced this (47 existing auth cases passed). A shared publication helper now registers cleanup barriers first, updates the auth-event owner, then publishes the session with a matching profile or no profile. Login, registration, recovery, startup restore and changed-account SDK callbacks share it. Same-account SDK refresh retains its prior behavior. The SDK callback stays synchronous.

Four new regression cases cover login, registration, startup restore and SDK callbacks, observing both login-handler ordering and every new-session store notification. Full suite passed 1,323 tests / 116 suites; TypeScript passed. ESLint reported zero errors and 38 existing-style warnings in the touched files. No auth API, database schema or production data was changed. Tests mock SDK calls and the cache-reset implementation, so they prove publication ordering rather than actual SDK persistence or native UI lifecycle.

[Evidence](readiness-evidence/auth-session-publication.json). Delayed authentication results and already-issued cleanup overlapping later login remain open; this change does not claim to serialize those operations. Full 10,000 DAU acceptance remains unachieved.

### Actual SDK session race reproduction — 2026-10-17

The remaining authentication race is now demonstrated against the actual installed @supabase/auth-js 2.115.0 through createClient, using a fully intercepted synthetic HTTP transport and asynchronous in-memory storage. No live endpoint, production credential or customer account was used. A sequential sign-in/sign-out control passed. Three deliberately reordered cases failed the intended identity invariant: a pending password sign-in restored the old session after completed logout; an old password response overwrote a newer completed sign-in; and an old logout response removed a newly signed-in session. Recorded SDK events show the corresponding late SIGNED_IN/SIGNED_OUT notifications, so the risk exists below the app store and can survive app restart through SDK storage.

The diagnostic script returns exit code 2 when these invariants fail; this is a known failing readiness diagnostic, not a passing CI gate. It does not patch third-party SDK code. Repeat with `node scripts/validation/auth-session-races.mjs docs/reports/readiness-evidence/auth-session-sdk-races.json`. Source hash/version and per-case storage/event evidence are retained in [the report](readiness-evidence/auth-session-sdk-races.json). Script ESLint passed. Earlier publication-order fixes remain valid but do not resolve these asynchronous mutation races.

The next implementation must coordinate whole app authentication operations (including SDK writes/events and local/IAP cleanup), reject obsolete completions, preserve the latest user's intent and account identity, and handle slow operations without allowing abandoned destructive work to race a new account. A store-only late-response guard is insufficient because the SDK has already persisted the session and emitted its event before returning. Actual SDK regression cases and app-level lifecycle cases must both pass before claiming resolution. No application source, production state or installed binary changed in this diagnostic step; full-suite and native build results remain those of the prior publication fix. The goal remains active and unachieved.

Indefinitely stalled native work cannot safely be ignored merely to start a new account. Actual application integration, app lifecycle regressions and native UI validation remain required; existing binaries and production state are unchanged. The goal remains active.


### Integrate complete auth-store operation coordination — 2026-10-17

The tested queue is now used by initializeAuth, handleDeepLink, registration, password/Apple/Google sign-in, logout and account deletion. It surrounds complete actions, including session publication and awaited side effects. UI timeouts still allow local logout to complete, but timed-out SDK, IAP and persistence promises are retained by the queue; the next authentication waits for those underlying operations to settle. IAP identification from sign-in and startup is also retained. Queued deletion captures the selected owner at request time and rejects an intervening account change before invoking deletion. A separate navigator lifecycle revision prevents an initialization queued before disposal from later attaching an observer. SDK callbacks remain synchronous.

Regression evidence: ordered pending sign-ins followed by logout; no new sign-in until timed-out local cleanup actually finishes; queued deletion cannot target a replacement account; disposed queued initialization cannot attach. Three queue behavioral tests cover error continuation, caller deadlines and separately retained native work. The same updated queue passes all four actual-SDK synthetic cases. The raw-SDK baseline remains a diagnostic of unsafe uncoordinated use rather than a claim that the SDK was patched.

Existing overlap tests now exercise ordered public actions; adversarial direct-store identity changes retain the prior stale-completion guards. Test fixtures were corrected to reset unused one-shot SDK mock responses between cases: those leaked into later tests and caused retained unresolved work. Earlier interrupted test runs were intentionally stopped after diagnosing stale fixture assumptions, not restarted because an observation window expired. A new lifecycle test initially asserted no getSession calls, but unrelated wardrobe background work legitimately reads the session; the final assertion measures its intended invariant, no observer registration.

No production state or customer data changed, and no native UI/store-signing validation is claimed.

Limits remain explicit: native/browser flows have not been exercised through this queue; indefinitely stalled native/storage work safely holds later authentication but needs user-visible recovery handling; direct password update and external IAP operations are outside this store queue; reset helpers may still launch untracked writes and need a complete writer audit. Those limits prevent claiming all authentication races or the full 10,000 DAU goal solved. ### Password reset account binding and completion flow — 2026-10-18

Password reset previously bypassed the authentication queue and directly mutated the SDK user. It now calls the coordinated updatePassword action with the recovery form's original owner. The action verifies the selected session, SDK session and response user, and the queue rejects account or identity-revision changes before executing scoped password updates or dialog-triggered logout. Same-owner logout/re-login cannot revive an old queued password request. Passwords are not logged or persisted by this change.

The rendered form stays bound to its initial authenticated owner, guards repeated submissions synchronously, ignores old-account/unmounted completion and dialogs, and handles haptic failures separately. The previous success button navigated to Sign In while leaving the account authenticated; it now awaits logout bound to the recovery owner before navigating. An old success dialog cannot sign out a newer account. SDK errors remain available to the screen's existing localized same/weak-password feedback.

### Hold account publication behind reset writes — 2026-10-18

### Preserve debounced flush failures — 2026-10-18

Failure-path audit found that createDebouncedStorage.flush waited on internal continuation promises which intentionally swallowed errors. A background write failure could disappear before a later flush, and a flush started during an in-flight write could incorrectly succeed after it failed. Conversely, a pending-key failure could reject flush immediately while another key still wrote in the background.

The storage adapter now retains the latest failed write/removal per key, clears that key's failure only after a successful write/removal, drains captured pending and in-flight work with allSettled, and reports any unresolved key failure after the drain. Queue continuation remains usable after rejection. This establishes an honest persistence acknowledgment rather than silently treating a failed write as complete.

Three new regression cases reproduced all three failures with seven existing cases passing before the fix. Afterward, all ten storage cases and the full suite (1,353 tests / 118 suites) passed, TypeScript passed, and touched-file ESLint passed without warnings. Existing same-key successful retry coverage still passes. [Evidence](readiness-evidence/debounced-flush-failure.json). No production state changed.

This is a lower-level prerequisite: reset/registry cleanup and the legacy upload service still suppress some errors, so end-to-end durable cleanup failure recovery remains unresolved. It does not prove data survives disk failure or that cross-account cleanup is complete under every failure. Raw writer/native/capacity/media/security/store/deferred-provider gates remain open; the goal stays active and unachieved.

Normal Android release build passed with the packaged debounced-storage source matching this change and the media audit marker absent. No native UI/store-signing validation is claimed.

### Distinct private thumbnail URLs expose cache-miss latency — 2026-10-18

Prepared two disposable accounts and 340 private objects, split into disjoint 170-URL remote/local cohorts. Each object contains identical 371,583-byte Pillow RGBA PNG bytes at 384×512, compression 6; it is a controlled thumbnail-sized fixture, not native codec output or varied user photos. Every issued URL is used once, so remote reads cannot warm the local cohort's URLs. Uploaded fixture bytes totaled 126,338,220. The temporary JWT-verified Edge probe also verifies the live fixture owner; anonymous and second-owner controls returned 401 and 403.

Remote execution in eu-central-1: 2 RPS completed 20/20 with p95 2,742 ms; 5 RPS completed 50/50 with p95 2,840 ms; 10 RPS issued/completed 81 of 100 planned, dropped 19 at the 20-request client cap, p95 2,780 ms. Local Mac: 2 RPS 20/20 p95 3,142 ms; 5 RPS 50/50 p95 3,206 ms; 10 RPS 75/100, 25 client-cap drops, p95 3,040 ms. All 296 issued reads returned HTTP 200, matched exact bytes/hash and reported CF-Cache-Status MISS. None timed out or failed after issue. Verified body bytes totaled 109,988,568.

Both ramps fail the configured no-drop acceptance requirement at 10 RPS. The 20-request cap belongs to the probe, so drops do not establish a backend hard limit; however, the roughly three-second cache-miss completion times contradict treating earlier warmed repeated-URL results as cold-media readiness. Header-resolution p95 was roughly 1.9–2.2 seconds, with body-plus-hash p95 roughly 1.0–1.15 seconds. These are fetch timing boundaries, not independently measured TCP/TTFB or separate hash timings.

Project metadata independently reports ap-northeast-1 (Tokyo), while the probe reported eu-central-1 (Frankfurt). Regional distance is a plausible contributor, not a proven cause. A fresh-cohort, controlled regional comparison is needed before changing placement. No existing function, bucket policy, image format, application transport or customer media was modified as a workaround.

Cleanup succeeded via Storage/Auth APIs and CLI function deletion. Independent SQL found zero fixture users, sessions, identities, profiles and objects; independent function inventory confirmed probe absence and the same 13 original function versions. The temporary JWT config and its temporary file are absent. [Detailed measurement and cleanup](readiness-evidence/distinct-media-ae276af3-eb51-4d81-9d00-c0420259ae1a.json), [summary](readiness-evidence/distinct-media-summary.json), [executed handler](readiness-evidence/distinct-media-handler-executed.mjs.txt), [entry](readiness-evidence/distinct-media-entry-executed.ts.txt), [executed orchestrator](readiness-evidence/distinct-media-orchestrator-executed.mjs.txt).

Harness validation passed 29 tests, including distinct-URL/no-reuse coverage and invalid/duplicate/excess cohort rejection; script lint passed. The executed orchestrator returned zero for successful execution/cleanup despite explicit remote/local passed=false; this is recorded, not treated as acceptance. A subsequent tooling correction makes future no-drop acceptance failure exit 2; this correction was linted but the live test was not repeated. No application implementation or installed binary changed, so the previous full app result remains 1,376 tests / 120 suites. The user-facing media latency/capacity gate and the overall 10,000 DAU goal remain unachieved. AI-provider work remains deferred.


### Tokyo/Frankfurt private-media comparison — 2026-10-18

Extended the bounded fixture harness for two fixed regional probes with fresh disjoint URL cohorts. Each probe uses the same 371,583-byte Pillow RGBA PNG fixture, owner/token, 2/5/10 RPS stages of ten seconds, twenty-request concurrency cap and fifteen-second deadline. Tokyo executes first, Frankfurt second; object cohorts were uploaded in that order. Runtime SB_REGION and x-sb-edge-region response headers must both match the requested region; the deployed entry also rejects execution outside its fixed expected region. The [official regional invocation documentation](https://supabase.com/docs/guides/functions/regional-invocation) specifies x-region and the response header used for this check. No application region/routing was changed.

Tokyo (ap-northeast-1, matching independently verified project region) completed 20/20, 50/50 and 100/100 with zero failures/drops. Stage p95 completion was 710, 633 and 718 ms; fetch-header-resolution p95 roughly 562–581 ms and body-plus-hash p95 127–149 ms. Frankfurt (eu-central-1) completed 20/20, 50/50 and 85/100; the final stage dropped fifteen scheduled reads at the client cap. Frankfurt p95 completion was 2,819, 2,773 and 2,768 ms, header resolution 1,886–2,022 ms and body-plus-hash 990–1,014 ms. All 325 issued reads returned HTTP 200, matched exact bytes/hash and reported CF-Cache-Status MISS, totaling 120,764,475 verified bytes.

The measured regional contrast supports placement as a contributor to this probe's latency. It does not prove that changing Edge placement will fix direct Storage image delivery to end-user devices, nor establish a backend hard throughput limit from client-cap drops. Fixed order, upload age, one asset/owner, short stages and absent native rendering remain limitations. No project migration, signed/public bucket change, existing-function redeployment or application transport workaround was made.

Anonymous/wrong-owner controls returned 401/403 in both regions. Both probes, all 340 objects and both test accounts were removed; independent SQL confirms zero users/sessions/identities/profiles/objects. Independent function inventory confirms both probe names absent and the same thirteen original function versions. Private JWT config and temporary config files are absent. The runner correctly exited 2 because overall no-drop acceptance was false, despite successful execution/cleanup. ### JPEG preview encoding, Android native verification — 2026-10-18

New previews of validated managed JPEG originals now use native JPEG quality 0.95. PNG/WebP/HEIC/HEIF originals retain PNG previews because those formats may contain alpha. This affects future preview generation; existing objects were not rewritten. Original bytes, owner/revision checks, pixel bounds, no-upscale and temporary-file cleanup protections remain unchanged. A focused test first reproduced JPEG originals unnecessarily producing PNG. All 25 focused tests now pass, as do TypeScript, changed-file ESLint and the full 1,381 tests / 120 suites.

An explicit audit APK ran the actual staging/thumbnail source with Expo native codecs on a fresh disposable Android 14 arm64 emulator, without a cloud session. Twelve cases passed: a repository PNG, a transparent icon, two repository-derived JPEG photos and all eight EXIF orientations. The two photo previews measured 85,077 versus 335,443 bytes (74.64% reduction) and 31,649 versus 101,287 bytes (68.75%) against same-native PNG baselines at identical 384×512 dimensions. Independent Pillow comparisons met preselected mean-channel-error <3 and PSNR ≥35 dB: 2.342/37.09 dB and 0.505/48.09 dB. ### Durable photo account cleanup and Android restart recovery — 2026-10-18

Confirmed that account cleanup previously removed persisted records/secure keys but never called durable photo deletion. Two new regressions failed against that source: cleanup neither awaited photo deletion nor reported its rejection. Cleanup now awaits native managed-photo removal before secure-key completion/marker acknowledgement. It deletes only the dedicated Documents/aiwardrobe-pending-media directory, covering previous owners and orphaned temporary files whose records were already removed. Web cleanup skips native filesystem access. The deletion shares the existing staging queue, checks the captured account revision before execution and after completion, propagates errors, and leaves subsequent retries usable. A stale queued cleanup cannot erase a newer account's files. Other cleanup stages still run after a photo-deletion failure, and the persistent cleanup marker remains until all required stages succeed.

All 43 focused tests pass, including root deletion scope, stale queued cleanup/new-owner preservation, and retry behavior. The full suite passes 1,386 tests / 120 suites; TypeScript passes. Changed-file lint has zero errors and 30 warnings (primarily existing lazy requires); the standalone native audit has two require warnings. The existing OS-filesystem/process-restart media durability harness also passes through the changed service, with temporary files removed. These are not complete UI or capacity checks.

A fresh disposable Android 14 arm64 audit used the actual cleanup source, real Expo filesystem and native AsyncStorage across PIDs 6174, 6289 and 6386, with confirmed force-stops between phases. Phase one injects a rejection at the JavaScript cleanup-service boundary and confirms the caller sees failure, marker remains and two owners' files plus an orphan persist. The fresh second process uses the unchanged real implementation: marker/files survive until retry, retry removes the managed directory, marker clears, and an unrelated Documents file remains. The third process confirms removals remain durable and clean startup does not recreate photos. All 19 assertions pass, zero fetch requests. An independent adb filesystem existence check also confirms the directory absent. This tests a service rejection and real native recovery, not an actual OS permission/locked-file/power-loss failure. iOS native recovery remains unverified.

The first audit attempt could not override Expo's read-only deleteAsync re-export and stopped before injecting its intended failure; its failed report/package are retained. The corrected harness injects at the service boundary, without changing application behavior. The emulator initially could not allocate its default partition due host disk space. Only this task's completed iOS build intermediates were removed; compiled app products and evidence were retained. The disposable Android data partition was sized at 2 GiB for tiny synthetic cleanup fixtures; this is not a storage-capacity test.

### Unpublished photo-save rollback — 2026-10-18

Confirmed a second durable-file leak during normal signed-in use: once an original was staged, preview failure or the post-staging wardrobe-limit check could reject without publishing an item/action, leaving files until account cleanup. Three regressions failed against the previous implementation. The item slice now records successfully returned staged files and removes them if preparation rejects before publication. This includes preview failure, missing supplied preview, account revision rejection and a limit reached during staging. Remote/canonical URLs are ignored by the existing managed-file deletion helper; newly allocated managed IDs isolate rollback from input/shared files. Rollback awaits all deletion attempts. If deletion fails, it reports photo_cleanup_failed with the preparation error as cause and cleanup errors attached, rather than falsely acknowledging successful cleanup. Once the item/action is published, sync errors do not roll back media: queued retries still need those files.

All 33 focused item-staging/media tests pass; full Jest passes 1,390 tests / 120 suites. TypeScript passes; changed-file/audit lint has zero errors and eight warnings (seven audit lazy-require warnings, one existing item-slice any). The four additional tests cover supplied-preview rejection, both files after a cap race, retained files/action after sync rejection and all deletion attempts plus explicit rollback-failure reporting; the existing preview-failure test now asserts original removal.

A separate iOS 26.5 native audit ran the actual item slice and media staging/deletion service with a repository JPEG fixture and synthetic owner, using real Expo filesystem calls. All 14 checks passed: original exists before an injected preview rejection then disappears with no item/action; a missing supplied preview leaves no files; a published save whose synthetic sync rejects retains both files and its action; filling the free-tier cap during staging removes both rejected-save files while preserving pre-existing item state. Six local file fetches occurred, zero external fetches. Preview production was replaced only in the audit to force branches/copy a preview; this is not a codec or real cloud sync/durability test. The in-memory fixture store does not stand in for production persisted queues. Native Android rollback parity remains open.

Probe anonymous/wrong-owner controls returned 401/403 in both regions. This run's acceptance passed and the runner exited zero.

The ten-RPS Frankfurt stage actually used more than twenty active reads, supporting the earlier cap-based drop explanation and demonstrating that the short offered workload can complete with more generator headroom. It does not establish an application latency improvement or a backend capacity ceiling, and separate runs/time/order are not a randomized causal trial. Frankfurt remains much slower than Tokyo.

### Same-render authentication submission admission — 2026-10-18

Reviewed the normal SignIn/SignUp handlers after the UI audit. They used loading state as the admission check, which is updated asynchronously. Captured callbacks invoked twice before a new render could submit the same pending auth action twice; a Google callback followed by an already-captured Apple callback could admit both. Email registration had no equivalent synchronous pending guard, and email sign-in checked only its loading state. The existing auth-store queue serializes admitted actions but does not discard these duplicate submissions. No claim is made that two native OAuth dialogs were observed: the reproduced issue is duplicated calls from the actual screen logic into mocked auth actions.

Each screen now uses a synchronous ref shared by its email, Google and Apple handlers. Provider handlers acquire it before setting loading state/calling auth; email handlers reject when occupied and acquire it after local validation. Every accepted handler releases it in finally after resolution/rejection, preserving explicit retry after cancellation. Existing loading/disabled presentation and the store's global auth-operation coordination remain intact. No provider routing, credentials, entitlements or service configuration changed.

Ten rendered regression cases exercise the actual two screens with mocked auth actions and callbacks captured in one React render. Before the change eight failed (duplicate Google, duplicate Apple, Google-to-Apple admission and duplicate email submission on each screen); two cancellation/retry controls passed. All ten now pass, as do both existing password-visibility regressions. Full Jest passes 1,402 tests / 122 suites; TypeScript passes. Targeted lint has zero errors and thirteen pre-existing screen warnings; the new test adds no warnings. These are screen logic tests with no real OAuth/account operation, not live provider or native rapid-tap verification.

### Recovery-email admission, accurate confirmation and unmount feedback — 2026-10-18

Reviewed ForgotPasswordScreen's direct recovery request. Four rendered regressions reproduced: same-render callbacks sent two requests; a queued input edit could make success identify a different address than the submitted normalized address; an auth error arriving after unmount showed a stale alert; and a retained callback could resend after success. A failure/retry control already passed. These tests use the actual component with a mocked Supabase auth method; no real email or account operation was performed.

The component now admits requests synchronously through idle/pending/sent state held in a ref. It captures the normalized submitted address before awaiting the SDK, disables editing while pending, and uses the captured address on confirmation. Pending failure returns admission to idle for explicit retry; acknowledged success remains sent so stale callbacks cannot resend. Mounted checks suppress late UI state, alerts and success feedback after unmount. They do not cancel an already-sent server request, and the guard is per component instance rather than a global cross-screen recovery queue.

Corrected confirmation copy in English/Russian/Uzbek. It now conditionally directs a registered-address user to check the displayed inbox, rather than claiming proven email delivery from an SDK acknowledgement. The new recoveryInstructions key replaces resetLinkSent in this screen and all three bundled locales.
